Queueing Setup in Figure
5

Figure
5
illustrates queueing systems where all jobs have interarrival and service times that are independently and exponentially distributed. Arrival and service rates can vary over time across three queueing settings.

In the base setting used for DRL policy training, the constant arrival rates for toxic comments are
\lambda_{\text{toxic}}=[4.2,2.9,3.4,5,2.7]
, and for non-toxic comments
\lambda_{\text{non-toxic}}=[12.4,6.1,22.1,33.4,7.8]
, corresponding to the groups:
white, black, male, female, and LGBTQ
. Service rates are also constant, where
\mu_{\text{toxic}}=[100,30,110,25,15]
and
\mu_{\text{non-toxic}}=[150,150,150,150,150]
for the same groups.

In Distribution Shift I, arrival rates for all 10 classes are modeled as time-dependent using a sinusoidal function:
\lambda(t)=\max(\lambda_{0}/2,\lambda_{0}+r\sin(\omega t))
, where
\lambda_{0}
is the baseline rate (i.e., the base arrival rate as in the base setting),
r=5
controls the amplitude, and
\omega=11
sets the frequency. This captures realistic fluctuations in comment volume due to daily cycles or trending topics. Service rates are constant and the same as the base setting to isolate the impact of arrival rate variability.

In Distribution Shift II, for all 10 classes, arrival rates are held constant as in the base setting, while service rates decrease over time to simulate system degradation. Service rates follow
\mu(t)=\max(d,\mu_{0}-kt)
, where
\mu_{0}
is the baseline service rate (the base service rate as in the base setting),
k
determines the rate of decline, and
d
is the lower bound. For toxic comments,
k=0
(no degradation); for non-toxic comments,
k=150
and
d=(2/3)\mu_{0}
(moderate degradation). This scenario reflects performance drops due to model drift, resource constraints, or increased workload.

Appendix B
Diffusion limits

We consider the following processes: partial sum process of interarrival time

U_{0}^{n}(t)
that depends solely on
\{u_{i}^{n}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
, partial
sum process of service time
V_{0}^{n}
and two other processes

\widetilde{Z}^{n}:=(\widetilde{Z}_{kl}^{n})_{k,l\in[K]},\widetilde{R}^{n}:=(\widetilde{R}^{n}_{l})_{l\in[K]}
that
solely relies on
\{(X_{i}^{n},Y_{i}^{n},v_{i}^{n}):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
, as well as the diffusion-scaled processes,
\bm{U}_{0}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n}:=(\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}^{n})_{k,l\in[K]},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n}:=(\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n}_{l})_{l\in[K]}
, as in Section
4
and Definition
8
to come.

As explained in Section
4
, our analysis begins by establishing joint diffusion limits of the primitive processes in Lemma
1
.
Deferring a detailed proof to
Section
B.2
, we highlight the main
ingredients of the joint convergence result. Our main observation is that the
diffusion-scaled processes admit a martingale central limit result when

\{(u^{n}_{i},v^{n}_{i},X^{n}_{i},Y^{n}_{i}):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
are i.i.d.
(Assumption
1
(i)) and

v^{n}_{i}\perp X^{n}_{i}\mid Y^{n}_{i}
(Assumption
1
(iii)). This allows us to show the weak convergence

\bm{U}_{0}^{n}\Rightarrow\bm{U}_{0}
in
(\mathcal{D},J_{1})
and

(\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n})\Rightarrow(\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})
in

(\mathcal{D}^{K(K+1)+1},WJ_{1})
. Since
\{u^{n}_{i}\}
and

\{(v_{i}^{n},X_{i}^{n},Y_{i}^{n})\}
are independent
(Assumption
1
(ii)), we can obtain the desired joint convergence (e.g.,
see
Whitt [71, Theorem 11.4.4]
which we give as Lemma
9
).

Building off of our diffusion limit, we can strengthen the convergence to the uniform topology using standard tools (e.g., see Lemma
6
and Lemma
7
), and conduct a sample path analysis where
we construct
copies
of
(\bm{U}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n})
and

(\bm{U}_{0},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})
that are identical in distribution with their
original counterparts and converge almost surely under a common probability
space. Abusing notation, we use the same notation for the newly construced
processes.

Lemma 4

(Uniform convergence)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
hold. Then, there exist stochastic processes

(\bm{U}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n}),~\forall~n\geq 1
and

(\bm{U}_{0},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})
defined on a common probability space

(\Omega_{\text{copy}},\mathcal{F}_{\text{copy}},\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}})

such that
(\bm{U}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n}),~\forall~n\geq 1
and

(\bm{U}_{0},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})
are identical in distribution with their
original counterparts and

\displaystyle(\bm{U}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n})\rightarrow(\bm{U}_{0},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})\quad\text{\hskip 10.22217ptin $(\mathcal{D}^{K(K+1)+2},\|\cdot\|)$,}\quad\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}}\text{-a.s.}.

(20)

We defer a detailed proof to Section
B.3
since it is a basic consequence of the Skorohod
representation theorem
[
6
, Theorem 6.7]
. Since the diffusion
limits
(\bm{U}_{0},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})
are multidimensional Brownian motions,
the copied processes of
(\bm{U}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n})
jointly
converge to a continuous limit almost surely. We obtain the
result by noting that convergence in
J_{1}
to a deterministic and continuous
limit is equivalent to uniform convergence on compact intervals (e.g.,
see
Glynn [24, Proposition 4]
stated in Lemma
7
).

Sample path analysis allows us to leverage properties of uniform convergence
and significantly simplifies our analysis. All subsequent results and their
proofs in the Electronic Companions, will be established on the copied processes in the
common probability space

(\Omega_{\text{copy}},\mathcal{F}_{\text{copy}},\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}})
with probability one, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}}\text{-}a.s.
, and
all of the convergence results will be understood to hold in the

uniform
norm
\|\cdot\|
.
Moreover, since these newly constructed processes are
identical in distribution with their original counterparts, all subsequent
results regarding almost sure convergence for the copied processes can be
converted into corresponding weak convergence results for the original processes;
see more discussion in Theorems
1

and
2
.

We use the uniform integrability condition in Assumption
3
to apply the martingale FCLT. For completeness, we review the martingale FCLT and Skorohod representation result before proving the main results of Section
3
.

B.1
Review of basic results

We review classical results on the martingale FCLT and the Skorohod construction.

B.1.1
Martigale Functional Central Limit Theorem

Our proof of Lemma
1
primarily relies on the
martingale FCLT
[
47
, Theorem 8.1]
. We define the maximum jump and
the optional quadratic variation of processes and review the martingale FCLT
in Lemma
5
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{[0,\infty)}:=\mathcal{D}([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
be the set of
right-continuous with left limits (RCLL) functions
[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
, and

\mathcal{D}^{k}_{[0,\infty)}:=\mathcal{D}([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{k})
be the product
space
\mathcal{D}_{[0,\infty)}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{D}_{[0,\infty)}

for
k\in\mathbb{N}
. With a slight abuse of notations, we also use
J_{1}
to denote
be the standard Skorohod
J_{1}
topology on
\mathcal{D}_{[0,\infty)}
and

WJ_{1}
to denote the product
J_{1}
topology on
\mathcal{D}_{[0,\infty)}^{k}
.

Definition 5

(Maximum jump)
.

For any function
x\in\mathcal{D}_{[0,\infty)}
, the maximum jump of
x
up to time
t
is represented as

J(x,t):=\sup\{|x(s)-x(s^{-})|:0<s\leq t\},\quad t>0.

(21)

Definition 6

(Optional quadratic variation)
.

Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be two martingales in
\mathcal{D}_{[0,\infty)}
with
respect to a filtration

\mathcal{F}\equiv\{\mathcal{F}_{t}:t\geq 0\}
satisfying

M_{1}(0)=M_{2}(0)=0
. The optional quadratic variation between
M_{1}
and
M_{2}

is defined as

[M_{1},M_{2}](t)=\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\big(M_{1}(t_{m,i})-M_{1}(t_{m,i-1})\big)\big(M_{2}(t_{m,i})-M_{2}(t_{m,i-1})\big),\quad t>0,

(22)

where
t_{m,i}=\min(t,i2^{-m})
.

Pang et al. [47, Theorem 3.2]
shows that
[M_{1},M_{2}](t)
is well-defined
for any martingales pairs
(M_{1},M_{2})
satisfying conditions outlined in
Definition
6
.

Lemma 5

(Multidimensional martingale FCLT)
.

For
n\geq 1
, let
\mathbf{M}^{n}\equiv(M^{n}_{1},\ldots,M^{n}_{k})
be a
martingale in
(\mathcal{D}_{[0,\infty)}^{k},WJ_{1})
with respect to a
filtration
\mathcal{F}_{n}\equiv\left\{\mathcal{F}_{n,t}:t\geq 0\right\}

satisfying
\mathbf{M}^{n}(0)=

(0,\ldots,0)
. If both of the following conditions hold

(i)

the expected maximum jump is asymptotically negligible:

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[J(M^{n}_{i},T)]=0,~\forall~i\in[k],~\forall~T\geq 0;

(ii)

there exists a positive semidefinite symmetric matrix

\mathbf{A}=\{a_{ij}\}_{i,j\in[k]}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
such
that for any
1\leq i,j\leq k
and
t>0
,

[M^{n}_{i},M^{n}_{j}](t)\Rightarrow a_{ij}t
in
\mathbb{R}
as

n\rightarrow\infty,

then, we have that

\mathbf{M}^{n}\Rightarrow\mathbf{M}\quad\text{ in }(\mathcal{D}_{[0,\infty)}^{k},WJ_{1})\quad\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,

where
\mathbf{M}
is a
k
-dimensional Brownian motion with mean vector and
covariance matrix being

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{M}(t)]=(0,\ldots,0)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{M}(t)\mathbf{M}^{\top}(t)]=\mathbf{A}t,\quad t\geq 0.

B.1.2
Skorohod representation

Recall the definition of random elements on a metric space
(S,m)

[
71
, Page
78]
.

Definition 7

(Random Element)
.

For a separable metric space
(S,m)
, we say that
\mathbf{X}
is a random
element of
(S,m)
if
\mathbf{X}
is a measurable mapping from some
underlying probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
to

(S,\mathcal{B}(S))
, where
\mathcal{B}(s)
is the Borel
\sigma
-field
induced by
(S,m)
.

The well-known Skorohod representation theorem
[
6
, Theorem
6.7]
gives the following.

Lemma 6

(Skorohod representation)
.

Let
\{\mathbf{X}^{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\mathbf{X}
be random elements of a
separable metric space
(S,m)
. If
\mathbf{X}^{n}\Rightarrow\mathbf{X}

in
(S,m)
, then there exists other random elements

\{\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\text{copy}}\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\mathbf{X}_{\text{copy}}
of

(S,m)
, defined on a common probability space

(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, such that (i)

\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\text{copy}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbf{X}^{n},~\forall~n\geq 1
and

\mathbf{X}_{\text{copy}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbf{X}
; (ii)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}m(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\text{copy}},\mathbf{X}_{\text{copy}})=0

\mathbb{P}
-almost surely.

Let
d_{J_{1}}(\cdot,\cdot)
be the
J_{1}
metric (Skorohod metric) defined on

\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
, the set of RCLL functions
[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

[
71
, Page 79]
. Moreover, for the
product space
\mathcal{D}^{k}:=\mathcal{D}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{D}
, let
d_{p}(\cdot,\cdot)
be the
product metric defined by

d_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\sum_{i=1}^{K}d_{J_{1}}(x_{i},y_{i}),~\forall~\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in D^{k}

[
71
, Page 83]
. It is known
that both
(\mathcal{D},d_{J_{1}}(\cdot,\cdot))
and
(\mathcal{D}^{k},d_{p}(\cdot,\cdot))
are
separable metric spaces with
J_{1}
topology and
WJ_{1}
(weak
J_{1}
) topology, respectively
[
71
, Sections 3.3, 11.4, and 11.5]
. Then, according to Lemma
6
, for weakly converging random elements, we can obtain copies
that converges almost surely. This enables us to conduct sample path
analysis. Specifically, if the limiting random element is continuous almost
surely, we can utilize the following theorem from
[
24
, Proposition 4]

to conduct analysis under uniform norm convergence, which can streamline our
analysis significantly.

Lemma 7
.

For a sequence of functions
X^{n}\in\mathcal{D}
,
convergence to a continuous function, say

X\in\mathcal{C}
, in the
J_{1}
metric

d_{J_{1}}(\cdot,\cdot)
is equivalent to convergence in uniform norm

\|\cdot\|
, i.e.,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}d_{J_{1}}(X^{n},X)=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|X^{n}-X\|=0.

B.2
Proof of Lemma
1

First, we define arrival and service processes of predicted classes on which
we apply the martigale FCLT to establish their weak convergence.

Definition 8

(Arrival and service processes of predicted classes I)
.

Given a classifier
f_{\theta}
and a sequence of queueing systems, we define
the following for a given system
n
and time
t\in[0,n]
:

(i)

(Counting process) for any real class
k\in[K]
and predicted class

l\in[K]
, let
\widetilde{Z}_{kl}^{n}(t)
be the total number of jobs from real class

k
predicted as class
l
, among the first
\lfloor t\rfloor
jobs
arriving in the system, i.e.,

\widetilde{Z}_{kl}^{n}(t):=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}Y_{ik}^{n}\widetilde{Y}_{il}^{n},~\forall~t\in[0,n];

Moreover, let
\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n}=\{\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}^{n}\}_{k,l\in[K]}
be the corresponding
diffusion-scaled process, defined as

\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}^{n}(t)=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}Y_{ik}^{n}\widetilde{Y}_{il}^{n}-p_{k}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}\cdot nt\Big],~\forall~t\in[0,1];

(ii)

(Cumulative service time) for any predicted class
l\in[K]
, let

\widetilde{R}_{l}^{n}
be the total service time requested by jobs predicted as class

l
, among the first
\lfloor t\rfloor
jobs arriving in the system, i.e.,

\widetilde{R}_{l}^{n}(t):=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}\widetilde{Y}_{il}^{n}v_{i}^{n},~\forall~t\in[0,n].

Moreover, let
\bm{\widetilde{R}}=\{\bm{\widetilde{R}}_{l}\}_{l\in[K]}
be the corresponding
diffusion-scaled process, defined as

\bm{\widetilde{R}}_{l}^{n}(t)=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\widetilde{Y}_{il}^{n}v_{i}^{n}-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}\cdot nt\Big],\quad~\forall~t\in[0,1].

We define
\widetilde{Z}_{kl}^{n}
and
\widetilde{R}^{n}_{l}
on
[0,n]
, and

\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}^{n}
and
\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n}_{l}
on
[0,1]
for analysis simplicity, and
these processes can be naturally extended to
[0,+\infty)
to apply the martingale FCLT
in Lemma
5
. In addition,
we introduce the following rescaled and centered processes
\bm{\widehat{U}}^{n}_{0}(t)
and

(\bm{\widehat{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widehat{R}}^{n},\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0}^{n})
to facilitate the analysis.

Definition 9

(Arrival and service processes of predicted classes
II)
.

Given a classifier
f_{\theta}
and a
sequence of queueing systems, we define the rescaled and centered processes
for a given system
n
and time
t\in[0,1]
as followings:

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}^{n}(t)=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}(u_{i}^{n}-(\lambda^{n})^{-1}),\quad

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0}^{n}(t)=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\Big[v_{i}^{n}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}\Big],

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{Z}}_{kl}^{n}(t)=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}[Y_{ik}^{n}\widetilde{Y}_{il}^{n}-p_{k}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}],~\forall~k,l\in[K],

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{R}}_{l}^{n}(t)=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\Big[\widetilde{Y}_{il}^{n}v_{i}^{n}-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}\Big],~\forall~l\in[K].

One can check that
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widehat{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widehat{R}}^{n}
are closely
related to
\bm{U}_{0}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n}
by noting that for any

t\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\bm{U}_{0}^{n}(t)=\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}^{n}(t)+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\lambda^{n})^{-1}(\lfloor nt\rfloor-nt),

\displaystyle\bm{V}_{0}^{n}(t)=\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0}^{n}(t)+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}(\lfloor nt\rfloor-nt),

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}^{n}(t)=\bm{\widehat{Z}}_{kl}^{n}(t)+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}p_{k}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}(\lfloor nt\rfloor-nt),

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{R}}_{l}^{n}(t)=\bm{\widehat{R}}_{l}^{n}(t)+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}(\lfloor nt\rfloor-nt).

Under Assumptions
1

and
3
, we establish the weak convergence
of
\bm{\widehat{U}}^{n}_{0}(t)
and
(\bm{\widehat{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widehat{R}}^{n},\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0}^{n})
using the martingale FCLT in Lemma
5
.

Lemma 8

(Individual weak convergence)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
hold. Then, there exist Brownian motions
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}
and

(\bm{\widehat{Z}},\bm{\widehat{R}},\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0})
such that (i)
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}^{n}\Rightarrow\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}
in

(\mathcal{D},J_{1})
; (ii)

(\bm{\widehat{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widehat{R}}^{n},\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0}^{n})\Rightarrow(\bm{\widehat{Z}},\bm{\widehat{R}},\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0})
in

(\mathcal{D}^{K(K+1)+1},WJ_{1})
.

We defer a detailed proof of the lemma to Section
B.2.1
.

The following processes are all well-defined deterministic functions of

t\in[0,1]
:

n^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\lambda^{n})^{-1}(\lfloor nt\rfloor-nt),~~n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}(\lfloor nt\rfloor-nt),~~n^{-\frac{1}{2}}p_{k}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}(\lfloor nt\rfloor-nt),~~n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}(\lfloor nt\rfloor-nt).

Assumption
2
and

n^{-1/2}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}(\lfloor nt\rfloor-nt)\rightarrow 0
imply that
all of them converge to
0
in
(\mathcal{D},J_{1})
. Using the jointly weak convergence
with a deterministic limit
[
71
, Theorem 11.4.5]
, continuity of
addition
[
71
, Theorem 4.1]
by almost-sure continuity of all limits,
and the continuous mapping theorem, it follows that there exist Brownian
motions
\bm{U}_{0}
and
(\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})
such that (i)

\bm{U}_{0}^{n}\Rightarrow\bm{U}_{0}
in
(\mathcal{D},J_{1})
; (ii)

(\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n})\Rightarrow(\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})
in

(\mathcal{D}^{K(K+1)+1},WJ_{1})
.

Since
\{u^{n}_{i}\}
and
\{(v_{i}^{n},X_{i}^{n},Y_{i}^{n})\}
are independent
(Assumption
1
(ii)), we can use the following
result
[
71
, Theorem 11.4.4]
to obtain our desired jointly weak convergence in Lemma
1
.

Lemma 9

(Joint weak convergence for independent random elements)
.

Let
\mathbf{X}^{n}
and
\mathbf{Y}^{n}
be independent random elements of
separable metric spaces
(S^{\prime},m^{\prime})
and

(S^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
for each
n\geq 1
. Then, there is
joint convergence in distribution

(\mathbf{X}^{n},\mathbf{Y}^{n})\Rightarrow(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\text{ in }S^{\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}

if and only if
\mathbf{X}^{n}\Rightarrow\mathbf{X}
in
S^{\prime}
and

\mathbf{Y}^{n}\Rightarrow\mathbf{Y}
in
S^{\prime\prime}
.

B.2.1
Proof of Lemma
8

To utilize the martingale FCLT (Lemma
5
), we extend

\bm{\widehat{U}}^{n}_{0}
and
(\bm{\widehat{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widehat{R}}^{n},\bm{\widehat{V}}^{n}_{0})
to

\mathcal{D}_{[0,\infty)}
and
\mathcal{D}_{[0,\infty)}^{K(K+1)+1}
,
respectively, and establish individual weak convergence for these extended
stochastic processes. We can get the desired result by restricting the
extended stochastic processes to the time interval
[0,1]
.

We establish weak convergence of the extended
\bm{\widehat{U}}^{n}_{0}
and

(\bm{\widehat{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widehat{R}}^{n},\bm{\widehat{V}}^{n}_{0})
separately. To show the former, note that

\{u_{i}^{n}:i\geq 1\}
are i.i.d. random variables with mean
(\lambda^{n})^{-1}

by Assumptions
1
. Evidently,

\{\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}^{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a martingale with respect to the natural
filtration and satisfies
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}^{n}(0)=0
. It thus suffices to validate the
conditions (i) and (ii) of Lemma
5
. To verify
condition (i), use the shorthand

\Delta^{n}_{i}:=|u^{n}_{i}-(\lambda^{n})^{-1}|
to write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{n}[J(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}^{n},t)^{2}]=n^{-1}\mathbb{E}^{n}\big[\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor}(\Delta^{n}_{i})^{2}\big]\leq\mathbb{E}^{n}\big[(\Delta^{n}_{1})^{2}\mathbf{1}\left\{(\Delta^{n}_{1})^{2}\geq\sqrt{n}\right\}\big]+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}.

From uniform integrability (Assumption
3
), we have

\mathbb{E}^{n}[J(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}^{n},t)]\leq\big(\mathbb{E}^{n}\big[|J(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}^{n},t)|^{2}\big]\big)^{1/2}\to 0
. To verify condition (ii), first truncate the triangular array
\{|u_{i}^{n}-(\lambda^{n})^{-1}|^{2}:i\in\mathbb{N},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
uniformly with a constant using the uniform integrability (Assumption
3
), and apply the triangular weak law of large numbers (WLLN)
[
16
, Theorem 2.2.6]
on the truncated array, with a choice of
b_{n}:=n
in that theorem, to obtain

[\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}^{n}](t)=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}(u_{i}^{n}-\lambda_{n}^{-1})^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}c_{u}t~~\mbox{where}~~c_{u}:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\rm Var}(u_{1}^{n})=\alpha_{u}-(\lambda)^{-2}.

We now show the weak convergnece of

\mathbf{G}^{n}:=(\bm{\widehat{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widehat{R}}^{n},\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0}^{n})
. We have

\mathbf{G}^{n}(0)=\mathbf{0}
and by Assumption
1
,

\{(Y_{i}^{n},X_{i}^{n},v_{i}^{n}):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
are i.i.d. and

X^{n}_{i}
is independent of
v^{n}_{i}
given
Y^{n}_{i}
. Therefore, by conditioning on

Y_{i}^{n}
, we have that for all
i\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{n}[Y_{ik}^{n}\widetilde{Y}_{il}^{n}]=p_{k}^{n}q_{kl}^{n},\quad\mathbb{E}^{n}[\widetilde{Y}_{il}^{n}v_{i}^{n}]=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}q_{kl}^{n},\quad\mathbb{E}^{n}[v_{i}^{n}]=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}},

which indicates that
\mathbf{G}^{n}
is a martingale with respect to the
natural filtration. To apply Lemma
5
towards

\mathbf{G}^{n}
, we now validate its conditions (i) and (ii). Using a similar argument as above, uniform integrability yields condition (i) of Lemma
5

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{n}[|J(\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0}^{n},t)|]\rightarrow 0,\qquad\mathbb{E}^{n}[J(\bm{\widehat{Z}}_{kl}^{n},t)]\rightarrow 0,\qquad\mathbb{E}^{n}[J(\bm{\widehat{R}}_{l}^{n},t)]\rightarrow 0

(23)

for all
k,l\in[K]
.
Similarly, the triangular WLLN gives condition (ii)

\displaystyle[\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0}^{n}](t)\Rightarrow c_{v}t,\quad

\displaystyle[\bm{\widehat{Z}}_{kl}^{n},\bm{\widehat{Z}}_{rs}^{n}](t)\Rightarrow c_{(k,l),(r,s)}t,\quad

\displaystyle[\bm{\widehat{R}}_{l}^{n},\bm{\widehat{R}}_{s}^{n}](t)\Rightarrow c_{l,s}t,

\displaystyle[\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widehat{Z}}_{kl}^{n}](t)\Rightarrow c_{0,k,l}t,\quad

\displaystyle[\bm{\widehat{V}}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widehat{R}}_{l}^{n}](t)\Rightarrow c_{0,l}t,\quad

\displaystyle[\bm{\widehat{Z}}_{kl}^{n},\bm{\widehat{R}}_{s}^{n}](t)\Rightarrow c_{k,l,s}t,

where

\displaystyle c_{v}:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}\alpha_{v,k}-(\sum_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}/\mu_{k})^{2},

\displaystyle c_{(k,l),(r,s)}:=\Big\{\begin{array}[]{lr}p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}(1-p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl})&\text{if }(k,l)=(r,s),\\
-p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}p_{r}\widetilde{q}_{rs}&\text{if }(k,l)\neq(r,s),\end{array}

\displaystyle c_{l,s}:=\begin{cases}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}\alpha_{v,k}-\big(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}}{\mu_{k}}\big)^{2}&\text{if }l=s,\\
-\big(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}}{\mu_{k}}\big)(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{ks}}{\mu_{k}}\big)&\text{if }l\neq s,\end{cases}

\displaystyle c_{0,k,l}:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}}{\mu_{k}}-\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}}{\mu_{k}}\Big)\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}\Big),

\displaystyle c_{0,l}:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}\alpha_{v,k}-\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}}{\mu_{k}}\Big)\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}}{\mu_{k}}\Big),

\displaystyle c_{k,l,s}:=\begin{cases}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}}{\mu_{k}}-\big(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}\big)(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}}{\mu_{k}}\big)&\text{if }l=s,\\
-\big(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}\big)(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{ks}}{\mu_{k}}\big)&\text{if }l\neq s.\end{cases}

B.3
Proof of Lemma
4

From the Skorohod representation (Lemma
6
), there exist stochastic processes defined on some common probability space

(\Omega_{\text{copy}},\mathcal{F}_{\text{copy}},\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}})
,

(\bm{U}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n}),~\forall~n\geq 1
and

(\bm{U}_{0},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})
, such
that
(\bm{U}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n})
and

(\bm{U}_{0},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})
are identical in distribution to their
original counterparts and

\displaystyle(\bm{U}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n})\rightarrow(\bm{U}_{0},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})\quad\text{\quad in $(\mathcal{D}^{K(K+1)+2},WJ_{1})$,}\quad\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}}\text{-a.s.}.

Or equivalently, with probability one

d_{p}\big((\bm{U}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n}),(\bm{U}_{0},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})\big)\rightarrow 0,

where
d_{p}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the product
J_{1}
metric. By definition of

d_{p}(\cdot,\cdot)
, each coordinate of

(\bm{U}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n})
converges to the limiting process in

(\mathcal{D},J_{1})

\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}}
-a.s.. Since

(\bm{U}_{0},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})
is a multidimensional Brownian motion,

(\bm{U}_{0},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{0})
is continuous

\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}}
-a.s.. By Lemma
7
,
\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}}
-almost surely, every coordinate of

(\bm{U}_{0}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{0}^{n})
converges to the limiting process in

(\mathcal{D},\|\cdot\|)
. This completes our proof.

. Then, we provide a sequence of interim results required for us to prove Proposition

, let
A_{0}^{n}(t):=\max\{m:U_{0}^{n}(m)\leq t\},~\forall~t\in[0,n]
be the total number of jobs that arrive in the system up to time
t
, and

\bm{A}_{0}^{n}(t)=n^{-1/2}\big[A_{0}^{n}(nt)-\lambda^{n}nt\big],~t\in[0,1].

(24)

By Lemma
4
,

\bm{U}^{n}_{0}\rightarrow\bm{U}_{0}\in\mathcal{C}
; since the limiting function is
continuous, convergence in weak
M_{2}
topology is equivalent to convergence in

[

. Using the asymptotic

[

follows
from convergence of
\bm{U}_{0}^{n}
.

Suppose that Assumptions

C.1
Convergence of arrival and service processes of predicted classes

We formally define the arrival and service processes associated predicted
classes, and provide corresponding diffusion limits in Proposition
3
. Given a classifier
f_{\theta}
,
suppose that Assumption
2
holds and consider
system
n
operating in
t\in[0,n]
. For
j\in\mathbb{N},
let
\widetilde{u}_{l,j}^{n}
and

\widetilde{v}_{l,j}^{n}
be the interarrival and service times of the
j
th arriving job in predicted class
l\in[K]
.

Definition 10

(Arrival and service processes of predicted classes II)
.

(i)

(Arrival Process) Let

\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(t):=\sum_{i=1}^{A^{n}_{0}(t)}Y_{ik}^{n}\widetilde{Y}_{il}^{n}
be the
number of jobs from real class
k
predicted as class
l
among jobs
arriving up to time
t\in[0,n]
,
and

\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{kl}^{n}(t)=n^{-1/2}[\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(nt)-n\lambda^{n}p_{k}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}t]~t\in[0,1]
be the diffusion-scaled process. Let

\widetilde{A}_{l}^{n}(t):=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{A^{n}_{0}(t)}\widetilde{Y}^{n}_{il}
be the number of jobs predicted as class
l
among jobs arriving
up to time
t\in[0,n]
,
and

\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{l}^{n}(t):=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{kl}^{n}(t)=n^{-1/2}\Big[\widetilde{A}_{l}^{n}(nt)-n\widetilde{\lambda}^{n}_{l}t\Big]
with
t\in[0,1]
be the diffusion-scaled
process. Here,
\widetilde{\lambda}^{n}_{l}:=\lambda^{n}\widetilde{p}_{l}^{n}
and
\widetilde{\lambda}_{l}:=\lambda\widetilde{p}_{l}
, where the occurrence of predicted class
l
and its limit are denoted by

\widetilde{p}_{l}^{n}:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}
and

\widetilde{p}_{l}:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}
.

(ii)

(Sum of Interarrival Time) Let

\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(t):=\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}\widetilde{u}_{l,j}^{n},~t\in[0,n]
be
the sum of interarrival times among the first
\lfloor t\rfloor
jobs
predicted as class
l
,
and

\bm{\widetilde{U}}_{l}^{n}(t)=n^{-1/2}\big[\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(nt)-n(\widetilde{\lambda}_{l}^{n})^{-1}t\big],~t\in[0,1]
be the corresponding diffusion-scaled process.

(iii)

(Sum of Service Time) Let

\widetilde{V}_{l}^{n}(t):=\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}\widetilde{v}_{l,j}^{n},~t\in[0,n]
be
the sum of service times among the first
\lfloor t\rfloor
jobs predicted
as class
l
,
and

\bm{\widetilde{V}}_{l}^{n}(t)=n^{-1/2}\Big[\widetilde{V}_{l}^{n}(nt)-n(\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{l})^{-1}t\Big],~t\in[0,1],
be the corresponding diffusion-scaled process. Here,
(\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{l})^{-1}:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}}{\widetilde{p}_{l}^{n}}\frac{1}{\mu_{k}^{n}}
and
(\widetilde{\mu}_{l})^{-1}:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}}{\widetilde{p}_{l}}\frac{1}{\mu_{k}}
are the expected service times of an arbitrary job predicted as class
l
and its limit.

(iv)

(Service Process) Let
\widetilde{S}_{l}^{n}(t):=\max\{j\in\mathbb{N}:\widetilde{V}^{n}_{l}(j)\leq t\},\;t\in[0,n],
denote the number of predicted class
l
jobs that have been served and departed after
t
units of service have been allocated to that class, and let

\bm{\widetilde{S}}_{l}^{n}:=n^{-1/2}[\widetilde{S}_{l}^{n}(nt)-n\widetilde{\mu}_{l}^{n}t],~t\in[0,1]
be the
corresponding diffusion-scaled process.

For simplicity, we also use the vector processes

{\bm{\widetilde{A}}}^{n}=(\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{l}^{n})_{l}
,
\bm{\widetilde{U}}^{n}=(\bm{\widetilde{U}}_{l}^{n})_{l}
,
\bm{\widetilde{S}}^{n}=(\bm{\widetilde{S}}_{l}^{n})_{l}
, and
\bm{\widetilde{V}}^{n}=(\bm{\widetilde{V}}_{l}^{n})_{l}
to denote the second-order/diffusion-scaled processes.

Proposition
3
plays a
major role in our analysis of the endogenous processes in
Section
C.3
. We use the little-o notation
o_{n}(1)
to denote uniform
convergence over
t\in[0,1]
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
.

Proposition 3

(Convergence of exogenous processes of predicted classes)
.

Given a classifier
f_{\theta}
, suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
hold. There is a Brownian motion

({\bm{\widetilde{A}}},\bm{\widetilde{U}},\bm{\widetilde{S}},\bm{\widetilde{V}})
such that

\displaystyle({\bm{\widetilde{A}}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{U}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{S}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{V}}^{n})\rightarrow({\bm{\widetilde{A}}},\bm{\widetilde{U}},\bm{\widetilde{S}},\bm{\widetilde{V}}),

(26)

and for any predicted class
l\in[K]

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{l}^{n}(nt)=n\widetilde{\lambda}^{n}_{l}t+n^{1/2}\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{l}^{n}(t)+o_{n}(n^{1/2}),

(27)

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(nt)=n(\widetilde{\lambda}_{l})^{-1}t+n^{1/2}\bm{\widetilde{U}}_{l}^{n}(t)+o_{n}(n^{1/2}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{l}^{n}(nt)=n\widetilde{\mu}_{l}^{n}t+n^{1/2}\bm{\widetilde{S}}_{l}^{n}(t)+o_{n}(n^{1/2}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{l}^{n}(nt)=n(\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{l})^{-1}t+n^{1/2}\bm{\widetilde{V}}_{l}^{n}(t)+o_{n}(n^{1/2}),

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\sup\limits_{1\leq j\leq\widetilde{A}_{l}^{n}(n)}\widetilde{u}_{l,j}^{n}\rightarrow 0,\qquad n^{-1/2}\sup\limits_{1\leq j\leq\widetilde{A}_{l}^{n}(n)}\widetilde{v}_{l,j}^{n}\rightarrow 0.

(28)

Proof.

Recalling that
\bm{A}_{0}
,
\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}
are Brownian motions (
25
), we begin by showing the limit

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{kl}^{n}\rightarrow\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{kl}:=\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}\circ\lambda e+p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}\bm{A}_{0},\quad\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{l}^{n}\rightarrow\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{l}:=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{kl}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}\circ\lambda e+\widetilde{p}_{l}\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{0},

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{U}}_{l}^{n}\rightarrow\bm{\widetilde{U}}_{l}:=-\Big(\lambda\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}\Big)^{-1}\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{l}\Big(\big(\lambda\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}\big)^{-1}e\Big).

Recall from Definition
8
and Definition
10
that

\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}=\widetilde{Z}_{kl}^{n}\circ A_{0}^{n}
and

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{kl}^{n}(t)=n^{-1/2}[\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(nt)-\lambda^{n}p_{k}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}nt]=\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}^{n}(n^{-1}A_{0}^{n}(nt))+p_{k}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}\bm{A}_{0}^{n}(t).

Since
n^{-1}A^{n}_{0}(n\cdot)\to\lambda e
by Lemma
10
, continuity of the composition
function
[
71
, Theorem 13.2.1]
and the continuous mapping theorem
yields

\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{kl}^{n}\rightarrow\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}\circ\lambda e+p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}\bm{A}_{0}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{l}^{n}\rightarrow\bm{\widetilde{A}}_{l}.

Since all limit have continuous sample paths, convergence in weak
M_{2}

topology is equivalent to uniform convergence
[
71
, Corollary
12.11.1]
. Asymptotic equivalence of counting and inverse processes
(with centering) gives convergence of
\bm{\widetilde{U}}^{n}_{l}

[
71
, Corollary
13.8.1]
.

Using a nearly identical argument, we show convergence of
\bm{\widetilde{S}}_{l}^{n}
and

\bm{\widetilde{V}}_{l}^{n}

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{V}}^{n}_{l}\rightarrow\bm{\widetilde{V}}_{l}:=\bm{\widetilde{R}}_{l}\circ(\widetilde{p}_{l})^{-1}e+\widetilde{p}_{l}(\widetilde{\mu}_{l})^{-1}\bm{\widetilde{M}}_{l},

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{S}}_{l}^{n}\rightarrow\bm{\widetilde{S}}_{l}:=-\widetilde{\mu}_{l}\bm{\widetilde{V}}_{l}\circ\widetilde{\mu}_{l}e=-\widetilde{\mu}_{l}\bm{\widetilde{R}}_{l}\circ(\widetilde{p}_{l})^{-1}\widetilde{\mu}_{l}e-\widetilde{p}_{l}\bm{\widetilde{M}}_{l}\circ\widetilde{\mu}_{l}e,

where
\widetilde{M}_{l}^{n}(t)
is the total number of job arriving in the system until
arrival of
\lfloor t\rfloor
jobs predicted as
l\in[K]
and
\bm{\widetilde{M}}_{l}^{n}(t)

is the corresponding diffusion-scaled process,

\displaystyle\widetilde{M}_{l}^{n}(t):=\max\Big\{m\geq 0:\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}\widetilde{Y}_{il}^{n}\leq t\Big\}=\max\Big\{m\geq 0:\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widetilde{Z}_{kl}^{n}(m)\leq t\Big\},~\forall~t\in[0,n],

(29)

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{M}}_{l}^{n}:=n^{-1/2}[\widetilde{M}_{l}^{n}(nt)-(\widetilde{p}_{l}^{n})^{-1}nt],~\forall~t\in[0,1].

(Recall
\widetilde{p}_{l}^{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}
.)
\widetilde{M}_{l}^{n}
is closely
related to
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widetilde{Z}_{kl}
and can be understood as a counting process
with “interarrival times” being
\{\widetilde{Y}_{il}^{n}:i\geq 1\}
.

Represent the service partial sum
\widetilde{V}_{l}^{n}
as a composition of
\widetilde{R}_{l}^{n}
with
the counting process
\widetilde{M}_{l}^{n}

\widetilde{V}_{l}^{n}(t)=\widetilde{R}_{l}^{n}(\widetilde{M}_{l}^{n}(t))=\sum_{i=1}^{\widetilde{M}_{l}^{n}(t)}\widetilde{Y}_{il}^{n}v_{i}^{n},~\forall~t\in[0,n]~~~~~~\mbox{(Definitions~\ref{definition: U, Z, R, V}
and~\ref{def: concerned processes for arrival and service})}.

Since
\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n}_{kl}\rightarrow\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}\in\mathcal{C}
, a similar argument
as before gives

\bm{\widetilde{M}}_{l}^{n}\rightarrow\bm{\widetilde{M}}_{l}:=-(\widetilde{p}_{l})^{-1}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}\Big)\circ\widetilde{p}_{l}^{-1}e\in\mathcal{C}.

From the continuous mapping theorem, we have the convergence of
\bm{\widetilde{V}}^{n}_{l}
and

\bm{\widetilde{S}}^{n}_{l}
.
∎

C.2
Dominance of p-FCFS and work-conserving policies

The results on the endogenous processes in Proposition
1
and the lower bound in Theorem
1
will be obtained assuming p-FCFS and work-conserving policies. We justify focusing on the set of p-FCFS and work-conserving policies.

p-FCFS

Given a queueing system
n
and a feasible policy, we can derive an associated feasible p-FCFS policy by swapping the service orders within each predicted class when the class has no previously preempted job. We show that the latter policy has stochastically smaller cumulative cost function
\bm{J}^{n}(t)
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
To do so, we analyze the distribution of the cost function under a modified data generating process governed by a new probability measure
\mathbb{Q}^{n}
such that the distribution of
\bm{J}^{n}_{\text{$\pi_{n}$}}
remains the same as the original one under
\mathbb{P}^{n}
. The idea is to define the classes of jobs that govern the cost functions and service time distributions to be invariant under permutation within each predicted class, and use the convexity argument on the cost functions.

We assume that under
\mathbb{Q}^{n}
,
\{(u_{i}^{n},X_{i}^{n},Y_{i}^{n},\widetilde{Y}_{i}^{n}):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
are generated in the same way as in Section
3
, but service times are generated differently. We introduce
\{(\widehat{Y}_{jl}^{n},\widetilde{v}_{jl}^{n}):j\in\mathbb{N}\}
that are indexed according to the
order of being served
rather than the order of arrivals within each predicted class
l\in[K]
. For
any
job that is served as the
j
th distinct job within predicted class
l
in system
n
, the service time is realized as
\widetilde{v}_{jl}^{n}
in a tuple
(\widehat{Y}_{jl}^{n},\widetilde{v}_{jl}^{n})
, where
\widehat{Y}^{n}_{jl}:=(\widehat{Y}^{n}_{jl,1},\ldots,\widehat{Y}^{n}_{jl,K})
denotes the one-hot encoding that determines the distribution of
\widetilde{v}_{jl}^{n}
as well as the cost function. In the sequel, we employ the subscripts
i
and
j
to signify indexing according to the arrival and service order within each predicted class, respectively.

We assume that for any queueing system
n
and predicted class
l\in[K]
,

(i)

\{\widehat{Y}_{jl}^{n},\widetilde{v}_{jl}^{n}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}
are i.i.d. random variables;

(ii)

\{\widehat{Y}_{jl}^{n},\widetilde{v}_{jl}^{n}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}
are independent of
\{(u_{i}^{n},X_{i}^{n},Y_{i}^{n},\widetilde{Y}_{i}^{n}):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

Note that when swapping service orders between jobs within each predicted class,
(\widehat{Y}_{jl}^{n},\widetilde{v}_{jl}^{n})
remains unchanged in each sample path in
\mathbb{Q}^{n}
, a key property to be utilized in our proof.
To connect with the original data generating process under
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, we define the distribution of
(\widehat{Y}_{1l}^{n},\widetilde{v}_{1l}^{n})
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}^{n}[\widehat{Y}_{1l,k}^{n}=1,\widetilde{v}_{1l}^{n}\leq x]

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{P}^{n}[Y_{1k}^{n}=1,v_{1}^{n}\leq x\mid\widetilde{Y}_{1l}^{n}=1],

(30)

for any
k\in[K],x\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathbb{P}^{n}[Y_{1k}^{n}=1,v_{1}^{n}\leq x\mid\widetilde{Y}_{1l}^{n}=1]=\frac{p_{k}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}}{\sum_{r=1}^{K}p_{r}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{rl}^{n}}\mathbb{P}^{n}[v_{1}^{n}\leq x|Y_{1k}^{n}=1]
by conditional independence between
v_{1}^{n}
and
\widetilde{Y}_{1l}^{n}
given
Y_{1k}^{n}
in Assumption
1
. Moreover, we use a modified (scaled) cumulative cost function
\bm{\widehat{J}}^{n}_{\pi^{n}}(t;Q^{n})
, where for any job that is served as the
j
th distinct job within predicted class
l
, the cost is incurred according to its “analytical class label”
\widehat{Y}_{jl}^{n}
and defined by
C^{n}_{k:\widehat{Y}_{jl,k}^{n}=1}(\cdot).

Lemma 11

(p-FCFS)
.

Given a classifier
f_{\theta}
and a sequence of feasible policies
\{\pi_{n}\}
, suppose that Assumptions
1

2
,
3
, and
4
hold. Then, for any queueing system
n
, there exists a feasible p-FCFS policy
\pi_{n,\text{p-FCFS}}
such that
\bm{J}^{n}_{\pi_{n,\text{p-FCFS}}}(t;Q^{n})\leq_{\text{st}}\bm{J}^{n}_{\pi_{n}}(t;Q^{n}),~\forall~t\in[0,1].

Proof.

Our proof uses a similar idea alluded to in the proof of
[
40
, Theorem 2]
and provides a rigorous justification. Given a feasible policy
\text{$\pi$}_{n}
in system
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can define an associated p-FCFS policy, say
\pi_{n}^{\prime}
, by applying the following basic operation: if there exists the
j
th arriving job in predicted class
l\in[K]
that starts to be served by
\text{$\pi$}_{n}
before the
i
th arriving job in the same predicted class with
\widetilde{U}^{n}_{l}(i)<\widetilde{U}^{n}_{k}(j)
and
i
being the smallest such index, then we swap service orders of the two jobs. It suffices to show that
\mathbb{P}^{n}[\bm{J}^{n}_{\pi_{n}^{\prime}}(t;Q^{n})>x]\leq\mathbb{P}^{n}[\bm{J}^{n}_{\pi_{n}}(t;Q^{n})>x]
for all
t\in[0,1],~x\in\mathbb{R}
.

We first claim that for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\bm{J}^{n}_{\text{$\pi$}_{n}}(t;Q^{n})
under
\mathbb{P}^{n}
has the same marginal distribution as that of
\bm{\widehat{J}}^{n}_{\text{$\pi$}_{n}}(t;Q^{n})
under
\mathbb{Q}^{n}
. That is,

\mathbb{P}^{n}[\bm{J}^{n}_{\text{$\pi$}_{n}}(t;Q^{n})>x]=\mathbb{Q}^{n}[\bm{\widehat{J}}_{\text{$\pi$}_{n}}(t;Q^{n})>x],~\forall~x\in\mathbb{R}.

(31)

The reason is that under
\mathbb{P}^{n}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{n}
, the actual service time and the true/analytical class label of a job that determines the cost function to be applied are not known until the job starts to be served. Moreover, given arrival times
\{\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(i):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
in predicted class
l\in[K]
, service times and true/analytical class labels of waiting jobs are i.i.d. as (
30
) under the two probability measures. The latter implies that given the same realization of
\{\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(i):i\in\mathbb{N},l\in[K]\}
, the conditional distributions of
\bm{J}_{\text{$\pi$}_{n}}(\cdot;Q^{n})
and
\bm{\widehat{J}}_{\text{$\pi$}_{n}}(\cdot;Q^{n})
are identical.

By (
31
), it suffices to show that
\pi_{n}^{\prime}
induced from
\pi_{n}
by the basic operation satisfies

\mathbb{Q}^{n}\big[\bm{\widehat{J}}^{n}_{\pi_{n}^{\prime}}(t;Q^{n})\leq\bm{\widehat{J}}^{n}_{\pi_{n}}(t;Q^{n}),~\forall~t\in[0,1]\big]=1.

(32)

To prove (
32
), fix a sample path under
\mathbb{Q}^{n}
in system
n
. Suppose that at some time
nt^{\prime}\in[0,n]
, there exist two jobs,
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
, that
arrived
as the
i_{1}
th and
i_{2}
th job in predicted class
l\in[K]
, respectively, with
\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(i_{1})<\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(i_{2})
, and have not been served at all. Suppose that
\text{$\pi$}_{n}
chooses to serve
i_{2}
at time
nt^{\prime}
as the
j_{2}
th distinct job served in predicted class
l
, and starts to serve
i_{1}
later as the
j_{1}
th distinct job in that class with
j_{1}>j_{2}
. Let
\Delta\widetilde{v}_{l}^{n}(j_{2},j_{1}):=\sum_{r=j_{2}+1}^{j_{1}-1}\widetilde{v}_{rl}^{n}
be the summation of service times for jobs in predicted class
l
that are served between
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
. Also, suppose
\widehat{Y}_{j_{1},l,k_{1}}^{n}=\widehat{Y}_{j_{2},l,k_{2}}^{n}=1
for some
k_{1},k_{2}\in[K]
. Note that
\Delta\widetilde{v}_{l}^{n}(j_{2},j_{1}),\widetilde{v}_{j_{2},l}^{n},\widetilde{v}_{j_{1},l}^{n},\widehat{Y}_{j_{1},l}^{n}
, and
\widehat{Y}_{j_{2},l}^{n}
are identical
regardless of which job is chosen for service
at time
nt^{\prime}
. Similarly, under the conditions on preemption in Section
4.1
, waiting times of jobs incurred by preemption during their service also remain the same independently of the job chosen for service at time
nt^{\prime}
. Let
\Delta\widetilde{w}_{l}^{n}(j_{2},j_{1})
be the summation of waiting times incurred by preemption on jobs served between
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
in predicted class
l
, and let
\widetilde{w}_{j_{1},l}^{n}
and
\widetilde{w}_{j_{2},l}^{n}
be the waiting times by preemption on
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
, respectively.

Now we are ready to show (
32
). We first show that changing service orders of
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
improves the cumulative cost at
t=1
. Specifically, the change
\bm{\widehat{J}}^{n}_{\text{$\pi$}_{n}^{\prime}}(1;Q^{n})-\bm{\widehat{J}}^{n}_{\text{$\pi$}_{n}}(1;Q^{n})
would be

\displaystyle\Big[C_{k_{2}}^{n}\big(t^{\prime}-\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(i_{1})+\widetilde{w}_{j_{2},l}^{n}+\widetilde{v}_{j_{2},l}^{n}\big)-C_{k_{2}}^{n}\big(t^{\prime}-\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(i_{2})+\widetilde{w}_{j_{2},l}^{n}+\widetilde{v}_{j_{2},l}^{n}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle-\Big[C_{k_{1}}^{n}\big(t^{\prime}-\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(i_{1})+\widetilde{w}_{j_{2},l}^{n}+\widetilde{v}_{j_{2},l}^{n}+\Delta\widetilde{w}_{l}^{n}(j_{2},j_{1})+\Delta\widetilde{v}_{l}^{n}(j_{2},j_{1})+\widetilde{w}_{j_{1},l}^{n}+\widetilde{v}_{j_{1},l}^{n}\big)

\displaystyle-C_{k_{1}}^{n}\big(t^{\prime}-\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(i_{2})+\widetilde{w}_{j_{2},l}^{n}+\widetilde{v}_{j_{2},l}^{n}+\Delta\widetilde{w}_{l}^{n}(j_{2},j_{1})+\Delta\widetilde{v}_{l}^{n}(j_{2},j_{1})+\widetilde{w}_{j_{1},l}^{n}+\widetilde{v}_{j_{1},l}^{n}\big)\Big]\leq 0,

where the inequality follows from convexity of
C_{k_{1}}^{n},C_{k_{2}}^{n}
and
\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(i_{1})<\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(i_{2})
, similarly to the proof
[
66
, Proposition 1]
. In fact, one can observe that the cost reduction holds true for all
t\in[0,1]
such that both jobs
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
are present in the system at time
nt
, and thus (
32
) follows. This completes our proof.
∎

Work-Conserving

For all system
n
and any feasible policy

\pi_{n}
, we can always create a work-conserving counterpart policy
by having the server during an idle time to serve any waiting job, if
available. Since preemption is allowed without incurring additional costs, the server
can pause service and come back to the preempted job later, ensuring that the
cumulative cost does not increase as stated in the following lemma; see further discussion in
[
66
, Section
2]
.

Lemma 12

(Work-Conserving)
.

Given a classifier
f_{\theta}
and a sequence of feasible policy
\{\pi_{n}\}
, suppose that Assumptions
1
and
4
hold. Then, for any queueing system
n
, there exists a feasible work-conserving policy
\pi_{n,\text{work-conserving}}
such that

\bm{J}^{n}_{\pi_{n,\text{work-conserving}}}(t;Q^{n})\leq\bm{J}^{n}_{\pi_{n}}(t;Q^{n}),~\forall~t\in[0,1],~\mathbb{P}^{n}\text{-a.s.}.

C.3
Convergence of the endogenous processes of predicted classes

To prove Proposition
1
, we formally define processes that are endogenous to
scheduling policies for predicted classes.

Definition 11

(Endogenous processes)
.

(i)

(Total workload process) Let
\widetilde{L}_{l}^{n}(t)
be the total service time
requested by all jobs predicted as class
l
and arriving by time

t\in[0,n]
, and
\bm{\widetilde{L}}_{l}^{n}(t)
be the corresponding diffusion-scaled
process,

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{l}^{n}(t)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{A_{0}^{n}(t)}\widetilde{Y}_{il}^{n}v_{i}^{n},~t\in[0,n],~~~\bm{\widetilde{L}}_{l}^{n}(t)=n^{-1/2}\Big[\widetilde{L}_{l}^{n}(nt)-\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}}{\mu^{n}_{k}}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}\cdot nt\Big],~t\in[0,1].

(ii)

(Cumulative total input process) Let

L_{+}^{n}(t)=\sum_{l}\widetilde{L}_{l}^{n}(t),~t\in[0,n]
be the cumulative total input
process and
\bm{L}_{+}^{n}(t):=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\bm{\widetilde{L}}_{l}^{n}(t),~t\in[0,1]
be the
corresponding diffusion-scaled process, i.e.,

\bm{L}_{+}^{n}(t)=n^{-1/2}\Big[L_{+}^{n}(nt)-\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}\cdot nt\Big],~\forall~t\in[0,1].

(iii)

(Policy process) Let
\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(t)
be total amount of time during

[0,t]
that the server allocates to jobs from predicted class
l
, and

\bm{\widetilde{T}}^{n}_{l}(t)
be the corresponding diffusion-scaled process,

\bm{\widetilde{T}}_{l}^{n}(t)=n^{-1/2}\Big[\widetilde{T}^{n}_{l}(nt)-\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}}{\mu^{n}_{k}}\widetilde{q}^{n}_{kl}\cdot nt\Big],~t\in[0,1].

(iv)

(Remaining workload process) Let
\widetilde{W}_{l}^{n}(t)
be the remaining service
time requested by jobs predicted as class
l
and present (waiting for
service or being served) in the system at time
t\in[0,n]
,

\widetilde{W}_{l}^{n}(t)=\widetilde{L}_{l}^{n}(t)-\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(t),\quad t\in[0,n].

(33)

and
\bm{\widetilde{W}}^{n}_{l}(t):=n^{-1/2}\widetilde{W}^{n}_{l}(nt),~\forall~t\in[0,1]
be the
corresponding diffusion scaled process.

(v)

(Total remaining workload process) Let

W_{+}^{n}(t)=\sum_{l}\widetilde{W}_{l}^{n}(t)
be the total remaining workload process
and
\bm{W}_{+}^{n}(t):=n^{-1/2}\sum_{l=1}^{K}\widetilde{W}^{n}_{l}(nt),~\forall~t\in[0,1]

be the corresponding diffusion scaled process.

(vi)

(Queue length process) Let
\widetilde{N}_{l}^{n}(t)
be the total number of jobs
that are predicted as class
l
and present (waiting for service or being
served) in the system at time
t\in[0,n]
, and
\widetilde{N}_{kl}^{n}(t)
be the
total number of true class
k
jobs that are predicted as class
l
and
present in the system at time
t\in[0,n]
. Let

\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{l}^{n}(t):=n^{-1/2}\widetilde{N}^{n}_{l}(nt),~\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{kl}^{n}(t):=n^{-1/2}\widetilde{N}^{n}_{kl}(nt),~\forall~t\in[0,1]
be the corresponding scaled processes.

(vii)

(Sojourn time process) Let
\widetilde{\tau}_{lj}^{n}
be the sojourn time, the time
span between arrival and service completion, of the
j
th job of predicted
class
l
. Let
\widetilde{\tau}^{n}_{l}(t)=\widetilde{\tau}^{n}_{l,\widetilde{A}_{l}^{n}(t)},~\forall~t\in[0,n]

be the sojourn time process where
\widetilde{\tau}^{n}_{l}(t)
denotes the sojourn time
of the latest job predicted as class
l
and arriving by time
t
and

\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}^{n}(t):=n^{-1/2}\widetilde{\tau}^{n}_{l}(nt),~\forall~t\in[0,1]
be the
corresponding scaled process.

Note that
\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}(\widetilde{U}^{n}_{l}(i))=\widetilde{\tau}^{n}_{l,i}
and
\widetilde{\tau}^{n}_{l}
only
exhibits jumps at arrival times
\{\widetilde{U}^{n}_{l}(i)\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
of jobs
predicted as class
l
. By definition,
\widetilde{\tau}^{n}_{l}
is also RCLL. Since

\widetilde{L}^{n}_{l}
is an exogenous process, according to Eq. (
33
), we can also characterize the policy process
\widetilde{T}^{n}_{l}
, or
equivalently, the scheduling policies, by the remaining workload process

\widetilde{W}^{n}_{l}
. The following results hold under p-FCFS feasible policies:

\displaystyle\widetilde{N}_{l}^{n}(t)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{A}_{l}^{n}(t)-\widetilde{S}_{l}^{n}(\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(t)),

\displaystyle\forall~t\in[0,n];

(34)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}(t)

\displaystyle=\inf\{s\geq 0:\widetilde{W}_{l}^{n}(t)\leq\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(t+s)-\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(t)\},

\displaystyle\forall~t\in[0,n];

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{l}^{n}(t)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}\left(t+\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}(t)\right)-\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(t),

\displaystyle\forall~t\in[0,n].

We show the convergence of the scaled input process
\bm{\widetilde{L}}^{n}_{l}
, which will be used to prove convergence of the workload
W_{+}^{n}
in Section
C.3.1
.

Lemma 13

(Convergence of scaled total input processes)
.

Given a classifier
f_{\theta}
, a sequence of queueing
systems, and a sequence of feasible policies
\{\text{$\pi_{n}$}\}
, suppose that
Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
hold. Then, for
any predicted class
l\in[K]
, we have that

\bm{\widetilde{L}}_{l}^{n}\rightarrow\bm{\widetilde{L}}_{l}:=\bm{\widetilde{R}}_{l}\circ\lambda e+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}}{\mu_{k}}\widetilde{q}_{kl}\bm{A}_{0},\quad\bm{L}_{+}^{n}\rightarrow\bm{L}_{+}:=\bm{V}_{0}\circ\lambda e+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}}{\mu_{k}}\bm{A}_{0}

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where
e
is the identity function on
[0,1]
. Also,
for any system
n
and time
t\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{l}^{n}(nt)=\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}\widetilde{q}^{n}_{kl}\cdot nt+n^{1/2}\bm{\widetilde{L}}_{l}^{n}(t)+o(n^{1/2});\quad L_{+}^{n}(nt)=\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}\cdot nt+n^{1/2}\bm{L}_{+}^{n}(t)+o(n^{1/2}).

Proof.

Note that
\widetilde{L}_{l}^{n}=\widetilde{R}_{l}^{n}\circ A_{0}^{n}
by Definition
11
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{L}}^{n}_{l}(t)=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\Big[\widetilde{R}^{n}_{l}(A^{n}_{0}(nt))-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}}{\mu^{n}_{k}}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}\cdot A^{n}_{0}(nt)\Big]+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}}{\mu^{n}_{k}}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}\cdot n^{-1/2}\Big[A^{n}_{0}(nt)-\lambda^{n}\cdot nt\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{R}}_{l}^{n}(n^{-1}A^{n}_{0}(nt))+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}}{\mu^{n}_{k}}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}\cdot\bm{A}^{n}_{0}(t).

Recall
\bm{A}^{n}_{0}(t)\rightarrow\bm{A}_{0}(t)
and
n^{-1}A^{n}_{0}(n\cdot)\rightarrow\lambda e
by Lemma
10
. Since
\lambda e
is continuous,
continuity of the composition mapping
[
71
, Theorem 13.2.1]
and
the continuous mapping theorem yields

\bm{\widetilde{L}}^{n}_{l}(t)\rightarrow\bm{\widetilde{R}}_{l}\circ\lambda e+\widetilde{p}_{l}(\widetilde{\mu}_{l})^{-1}\bm{A}_{0}.

Convergence of
\bm{L}_{+}^{n}
is a direct consequence of continuous mapping theorem and
\bm{L}_{+}^{n}=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\bm{\widetilde{L}}^{n}_{l}
by Definition
11
.
∎

C.3.1
Proof of Proposition
1

We establish Proposition
1
based on Proposition
3
and Lemma
13
. Our approach is similar to the proof of
[
66
, Proposition
2]
, and we complement the latter with additional details in the
proof. Since
\{\text{$\pi_{n}$}\}
is work-conserving, the remaining workload
process
W_{+}^{n}
can be written as

W_{+}^{n}=\phi(L_{+}^{n}-e)

[
71
]
, where
\phi
is the
one-sided reflection mapping. By Lemma
13
and
heavy-traffic conditions (Assumption
2
), for
any
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle(L_{+}^{n}-e)(nt)

\displaystyle=\Big(\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}-1\Big)\cdot nt+n^{1/2}\bm{L}_{+}^{n}(t)+o(n^{1/2})

\displaystyle=n^{1/2}\Big[\bm{L}_{+}^{n}(t)+n^{1/2}\big(\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}-1\big)t\Big]+o(n^{1/2})

\displaystyle=n^{1/2}\bm{L}_{+}^{n}(t)+o(n^{1/2}),

where we used

n^{1/2}\big(\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}-1\big)=o_{n}(1)
in the final line. Combining with the relation

W_{+}^{n}=\phi(L_{+}^{n}-e)
, we get

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}W_{+}^{n}(nt)=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\phi(L_{+}^{n}-e)(nt)=\phi(n^{-1/2}(L_{+}^{n}-e)(nt))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\bm{L}_{+}^{n}(t)+o_{n}(1))=\phi(\bm{L}_{+}^{n}(t))+o_{n}(1),

where the first line follows from definition of
\phi
, and the last line
results from Lipschitz property of
\phi
with the uniform metric
[
71
, Lemma
13.5.1]
. Since
\bm{W}_{+}^{n}(t):=n^{-1/2}W_{+}^{n}(nt)
in
Definition
11
, the convergence

\bm{W}_{+}^{n}\rightarrow\phi(\bm{L}_{+})
follows from the above and
Lemma
13
.

Next, we consider
\bm{\widetilde{W}}^{n}_{l},\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{n}_{l}
, and
\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}^{n}_{l}
.
Notice that
\bm{\widetilde{W}}^{n}_{l}\geq 0
,
\sum_{l=1}^{K}\bm{\widetilde{W}}^{n}_{l}\rightarrow\phi(\bm{L}_{+})
, and
\phi(\bm{L}_{+})
is a continous function on
[0,1]
. Therefore, it is clear that
\limsup_{n}\|\bm{\widetilde{W}}^{n}_{l}\|<+\infty,~\forall~l\in[K]
.
For
\bm{\widetilde{T}}^{n}_{l}
, by Definition
11
and Lemma
13
, we have that

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{T}}_{l}^{n}(t)=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\Big[\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(nt)-\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}\cdot nt\Big]

(35)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\Big[\widetilde{L}_{l}^{n}(nt)-\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}\cdot nt\Big]-n^{-1/2}\widetilde{W}_{l}^{n}(nt)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{L}}^{n}_{l}(t)-\bm{\widetilde{W}}^{n}_{l}(t),

where the second line follows from
\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(nt)=\widetilde{L}_{l}^{n}(nt)-\widetilde{W}_{l}^{n}(nt)
by Definition
11
.
Since
\bm{\widetilde{L}}^{n}_{l}\rightarrow\bm{\widetilde{L}}_{l}
by Lemma
13
, one can check that for any
l\in[K]
,
\bm{\widetilde{T}}_{l}^{n}
converges if and only if
\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}^{n}
converges. Also,
\limsup_{n}\|\bm{\widetilde{T}}_{l}^{n}\|<+\infty,~\forall~l\in[K]
.

Recalling the relation (
34
), we have

\widetilde{N}_{l}^{n}(nt)=\widetilde{A}_{l}^{n}(nt)-\widetilde{S}_{l}^{n}(\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(nt))
. Using
Proposition
3
, we can
rewrite
\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{n}_{l}(t)
as

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{n}_{l}(t)=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}[\widetilde{A}_{l}^{n}(nt)-\widetilde{S}_{l}^{n}(\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(nt))]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{1/2}\lambda^{n}\widetilde{p}_{l}^{n}t+\bm{\widetilde{A}}^{n}_{l}(t)-n^{1/2}\widetilde{\mu}_{l}^{n}t\cdot n^{-1}\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(nt)-\bm{\widetilde{S}}^{n}_{l}(n^{-1}\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(nt))+o(1).

Note that

n^{-1}\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(nt)=\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}}{\mu^{n}_{k}}\cdot t+n^{-1/2}\bm{\widetilde{T}}^{n}_{l}(t)+o(n^{-1/2}),

(36)

where
n^{-1/2}\bm{\widetilde{T}}^{n}_{l}(t)=o_{n}(1)
as
\limsup_{n}\|\bm{\widetilde{T}}^{n}_{l}\|<+\infty
.
Therefore,
\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{n}_{l}(t)
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{l}^{n}(t)=

\displaystyle~n^{1/2}\Big[\lambda^{n}\widetilde{p}^{n}_{l}\cdot t-\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{l}\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}}{\mu^{n}_{k}}\cdot t\Big]-\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{l}\bm{\widetilde{T}}^{n}_{l}(t)+\bm{\widetilde{A}}^{n}_{l}(t)-\bm{\widetilde{S}}^{n}_{l}\Big(\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}}{\mu^{n}_{k}}\cdot t+o_{n}(1)\Big)+o_{n}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~-\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{l}\bm{\widetilde{T}}^{n}_{l}(t)+\bm{\widetilde{A}}^{n}_{l}(t)-\bm{\widetilde{S}}^{n}_{l}\Big(\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}}{\mu^{n}_{k}}\cdot t+o_{n}(1)\Big)+o_{n}(1),

(37)

by definition of
\widetilde{p}^{n}_{l}
and
\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{l}
in Definition
10
. Since

\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}}{\mu^{n}_{k}}\cdot e\rightarrow\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}}{\mu_{k}}\cdot e\in\mathcal{C}
,
by continuity of composition
[
71
, Theorem 13.2.1]
and continuous mapping theorem, for any
l\in[K]
,
\bm{\widetilde{T}}_{l}^{n}
converges if and only if
\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{l}^{n}
converges, and
\limsup_{n}\|\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{l}^{n}\|<+\infty,~\forall~l\in[K]
.

Finally, for
\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}^{n}_{l}
, once again by (
34
), we have that

\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}^{n}(t)=n^{-1/2}[\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(nt+\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}(nt))-\widetilde{T}_{l}^{n}(nt)],~\forall~t\in[0,n].

According to the previous result (
36
), we have

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}^{n}(t)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}\cdot\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}^{n}(t)+\bm{\widetilde{T}}_{l}^{n}(t+n^{-1}\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}(nt))-\bm{\widetilde{T}}_{l}^{n}(t)+o_{n}(1).

(38)

For any predicted class
l\in[K]
,
\limsup_{n}\|\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}^{n}\|<+\infty
and

\limsup_{n}\|\bm{\widetilde{T}}_{l}^{n}\|<+\infty
, so that

\limsup_{n}\|\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}^{n}\|<+\infty
and
n^{-1}\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}(n\cdot)=o_{n}(1)
.
Moreover, for any predicted class
l\in[K]
,
since
\bm{\widetilde{T}}_{l}^{n}
converges if and only if
\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}^{n}
converges,
it is easy to show that if
\bm{\widetilde{T}}_{l}^{n}
and
\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}^{n}
converge,
then
\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}^{n}
converges. Also,
for the case that
\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}^{n}
converges in
(\mathcal{D},\|\cdot\|)
,
according to
\bm{\widetilde{T}}_{l}^{n}(t)=\bm{\widetilde{L}}^{n}_{l}(t)-\bm{\widetilde{W}}^{n}_{l}(t)
by (
35
), we have that

\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}^{n}(t+n^{-1}\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}(nt))=\lambda^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}}{\mu_{k}^{n}}\cdot\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}^{n}(t)+\bm{\widetilde{L}}_{l}^{n}(t+n^{-1}\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}(nt))-\bm{\widetilde{L}}_{l}^{n}(t)+o_{n}(1).

(39)

Since
\bm{\widetilde{L}}^{n}_{l}\rightarrow\bm{\widetilde{L}}_{l}\in\mathcal{C}
by Lemma
13
and
n^{-1}\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}(n\cdot)
vanishes, convergence of
\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}^{n}
in
(\mathcal{D},\|\cdot\|)
and the right-continuity imply convergence of
\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}^{n}
in
(\mathcal{D},\|\cdot\|)
. Finally, by Eqs. (
35
), (
37
), (
39
), if any of
\bm{\widetilde{T}}^{n}_{l},\bm{\widetilde{W}}^{n}_{l},\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{n}_{l}
and
\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}^{n}_{l}
has a continuous limit, so does the others.

C.4
Diffusion limits of the classical queueing model

We extend the classical queueing model in
Van Mieghem [66]
and
Mandelbaum and Stolyar [40]
in the presence of misclassification errors. Key convergence results analogous to Lemma
4
, Proposition
3
, and Proposition
1
can be shown similarly to our proofs. However, in this framework we can only
establish
optimality of P
c\mu
-rule among
p-FCFS
policies, a weaker guarantee than Theorem
2
, where optimality holds over all feasible policies. Whether P
c\mu
-rule remains optimal over all feasible policies in the classical model is left open.

C.4.1
Diffusion limit in the classical framework

We explain a new data generating process given external arrivals from
K
real classes as in
[
66
,
40
]
. For
k\in[K]
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, i.i.d. random vectors
\{(u_{ki}^{n},X_{ki}^{n},v_{ki}^{n}):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
are generated, where
u_{ki}^{n}
is the interarrival time of the
i
th arriving job of real class
k
in system
n
with a constant arrival rate
\lambda_{k}^{n}
,
(\lambda_{k}^{n})^{-1}:=\mathbb{E}^{n}[u_{k1}^{n}]>0
. The tuple
(X_{ki}^{n},v_{ki}^{n})
is generated
independently
of
u_{ki}^{n}
, where

X_{ki}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
represents the feature vector of the job, and
v_{ki}^{n}
indicates the time required to serve the job. Let

(\mu_{k}^{n})^{-1}:=\mathbb{E}^{n}[v_{k1}^{n}]
be the expected service
time of a class-
k
job. Let
U_{k}^{n}(m):=\sum_{i=1}^{m}u_{ki}^{n}
be the arrival epoch of the
m
th real class
k
job, and
A_{k}^{n}(t)=\max\{m:U_{k}^{n}(m)\leq t\},~t\in[0,n],
the corresponding arrival counting process of real class
k
.

For each
k\in[K]
, the predicted class of the
i
th real class
k
job is defined by the one-hot vector
\widetilde{Y}_{ki}^{n}:=f_{\theta}(X_{ki}^{n})=(\widetilde{Y}_{ki}^{n}(1),...,\widetilde{Y}_{ki}^{n}(K))
. The classification probabilities are defined as
\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}:=\mathbb{P}^{n}[\widetilde{Y}_{k1}^{n}(l)=1]
for
k,l\in[K]
. We assume that
v^{n}_{ki}
is independent of
X^{n}_{ki}
, implying
v_{ki}^{n}\perp\widetilde{Y}^{n}_{ki}
. The data generating processes and the corresponding heavy traffic conditions are summarized as the following.

Assumption 9

(Alternative data generating processes)
.

For any system
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(i)

the sequences of random vectors
\{(u_{ki}^{n},v_{ki}^{n},X_{ki}^{n}):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
are independent over
k\in[K]
;

(ii)

\{(u_{ki}^{n},v_{ki}^{n},X_{ki}^{n}):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a sequence of i.i.d random vectors for each class
k\in[K]
;

(iii)

\{u_{ki}^{n}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
,
\{v_{ki}^{n}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
, and
\{X_{ki}^{n}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
are independent for each class
k\in[K]
.

Assumption 10

(Heavy traffic condition)
.

Given a classifier
f_{\theta}
and a sequence of queueing systems, there exist

\lambda_{k},\mu_{k}\in(0,\infty)
and
\widetilde{q}_{kl}\in[0,1]
for
k,l\in[K]
such that

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widetilde{q}_{kl}>0,~\forall~l\in[K]
,

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\mu_{k}}=1
, and as

n\rightarrow\infty
, for all
k,l\in[K]

n^{1/2}\big(\lambda_{k}^{n}-\lambda_{k}\big)\rightarrow 0,\quad n^{1/2}\big(\mu_{k}^{n}-\mu_{k}\big)\rightarrow 0,\quad n^{1/2}\big(\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}-\widetilde{q}_{kl}\big)\rightarrow 0.

(40)

Under the identification
\lambda_{k}=\lambda p_{k}
, the critical-load
condition
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\lambda_{k}/\mu_{k}=1
coincides with

\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}/\mu_{k}=1
in Assumption
2
, so the two models share the same heavy-traffic scaling.

Diffusion limit

To derive the diffusion limit in the classical model, the key processes in Definition
8
are modified to

\widetilde{Z}_{kl}^{n}(t):=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}\widetilde{Y}_{ki}^{n}(l),\quad\widetilde{R}_{kl}^{n}(t):=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}\widetilde{Y}_{ki}^{n}(l)v_{ki}^{n},\quad t\in[0,n],~\forall k,l\in[K].

Note that
\widetilde{R}_{kl}^{n}
is now defined for each pair of
k,l\in[K]
. Then, using Assumptions
3
,
9
,
10
, the convergence results analogous to Lemma
1
and Lemma
4
can be obtained using the martingale FCLT (Lemma
5
) as in Section
B.2
and Section
B.3
. Building off of the initial diffusion limit, we can show convergence of the processes of predicted classes as in Proposition
3
and Proposition
1
using similar techniques. Specifically, let arrival processes associated with predicted classes be
\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(t):=\sum_{i=1}^{A_{k}^{n}(t)}\widetilde{Y}_{ki}^{n}(l),~\widetilde{A}_{l}^{n}(t):=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(t),~t\in[0,n]
for
k,l\in[K]
, and adapt the definitions of the other processes (Definition
10
, Definition
11
) and their characterizations analogously. For example, similarly to the proof of Proposition
3
,
\widetilde{V}_{l}^{n}
will have to be represented as a composition to apply the random time change technique:

\widetilde{V}_{l}^{n}(t)=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\widetilde{R}_{kl}^{n}\big((A_{k}^{n}\circ\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n})(t)\big)=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{A_{k}^{n}(\widetilde{U}_{l}^{n}(t))}\widetilde{Y}_{ki}^{n}(l)v_{ki}^{n},~t\in[0,n].

Apart from this per-pair decomposition of
\widetilde{R}_{kl}^{n}
and the composition above, every step coincides with the single-stream proofs, so the analogous convergence results hold
mutatis mutandis
.

C.4.2
Failure of the p-FCFS interchange argument under the classical queueing model

We show that the interchange argument behind the p-FCFS reduction (Lemma
11
) breaks down in the classical queueing model, so that our proof no longer reduces feasible policies to p-FCFS policies without loss. The obstruction is specific to misclassification. Under perfect classification (
Q^{n}=I
), each predicted class consists of a single real class with i.i.d. service times, Lemma
11
applies verbatim, and optimality over all feasible policies follows as in Theorem
2
. Under misclassification, a predicted class mixes several real classes with different service laws, and with class-specific arrival streams the arrival timing of a job is informative of its real class. As a result, service times of waiting jobs in a predicted class are not generally i.i.d. with respect to the filtration that policies are adapted to (Definition
1
), except for the special case of independent Poisson arrivals, where memoryless interarrivals render the timing uninformative. The proof of Lemma
11
is then inapplicable, and we can only establish the distributional lower bound (
8
) and the optimality in Theorem
2
over p-FCFS policies rather than all feasible policies. Whether P
c\mu
-rule remains optimal over all feasible policies in this model is left open.

To be concrete, consider a two-class system
n
where
\{u_{1i}^{n}\}
and
\{u_{2i}^{n}\}
take values of either
100
or
150
and
1
or
3
, respectively. Let service times
\{v_{1i}^{n}\}
and
\{v_{2i}^{n}\}
be either
2
or
6
and
\frac{1}{2}
or
\frac{3}{2}
, respectively, and
\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}=\frac{1}{2}
for all
k,l=1,2
. The service supports
\{2,6\}
and
\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}\}
are disjoint, so a revealed service time identifies the real class. Moreover, real class
k
arrival epochs lie in the additive semigroup generated by the support of its interarrival law, namely
\langle 100,150\rangle
for class
1
and
\langle 1,3\rangle
for class
2
. Suppose
predicted class

1
has two waiting jobs with arrival times
\widetilde{U}_{1,j}^{n},~j=1,2
.

Not identically distributed.
Consider
\widetilde{U}_{1,1}^{n}=100,~\widetilde{U}_{1,2}^{n}=103
. The first job may be of real class
1
or
2
, whereas
103\notin\langle 100,150\rangle
forces the second job to be of real class
2
. The two waiting jobs thus have different service-time distributions.

Not independent.
Consider
\widetilde{U}_{1,1}^{n}=100,~\widetilde{U}_{1,2}^{n}=150
. If the first job’s service time is
2
or
6
, then it is of real class
1
, and the second job cannot then be of real class
1
since
150-100=50\notin\{100,150\}
, so it is of real class
2
. If instead the first service time is
\frac{1}{2}
or
\frac{3}{2}
, the first job is of real class
2
and the second can be of real class
1
with positive probability. The conditional law of the second job’s service time therefore depends on the first.

Either failure invalidates the i.i.d. premise behind the proof of Lemma
11
.

C.5
Proof of Lemma
3

Using the shorthand

f^{n}_{kl}(s):=C_{k}^{n}(\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}(ns))=C_{k}^{n}(n^{1/2}\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}^{n}(s))
,

f_{kl}(s):=C_{k}(\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}(s))
,

\mathrm{d}\xi^{n}_{kl}(\cdot):=\mathrm{d}\left(n^{-1}\widetilde{A}^{n}_{kl}(n\cdot)\right)(\cdot)
,
and
\mathrm{d}\xi_{kl}(\cdot):=\mathrm{d}\left(\lambda p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}e(\cdot)\right)
,
triangle inequalities gives

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,1]}|\bm{J}^{n}_{\text{$\pi_{n}$}}(t;Q^{n})-\bm{J}_{\text{$\pi$}}(t;Q)|=\sup\limits_{t\in[0,1]}\Big|\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{t}f^{n}_{kl}(s)\mathrm{d}\xi^{n}_{kl}(s)-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{t}f_{kl}(s)\mathrm{d}\xi_{kl}(s)\Big|

(41)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{K}\left\{\sup\limits_{t\in[0,1]}\int_{0}^{t}\big|f^{n}_{kl}(s)-f_{kl}(s)\big|\mathrm{d}\xi^{n}_{kl}(s)+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,1]}\Big|\int_{0}^{t}f_{kl}(s)\mathrm{d}\xi^{n}_{kl}(s)-\int_{0}^{t}f_{kl}(s)\mathrm{d}\xi_{kl}(s)\Big|\right\}.

The first term of (
41
)
\rightarrow 0
since
f^{n}_{kl}(s)\rightarrow f_{kl}(s)
by Assumption
4
and
\lim_{n}\xi^{n}_{kl}([0,1])=\xi_{kl}([0,1])<+\infty

by Proposition
3
.
For the second term of (
41
), by Proposition
3
and generalized Lebesgue convergence
theorem
[
53
, Page 270]
, it is clear that

\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}f_{kl}(s)\mathrm{d}\xi^{n}_{kl}(s)-\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}f_{kl}(s)\mathrm{d}\xi_{kl}(s)\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
for any fixed

t^{\prime}\in[0,1]
. To achieve uniform convergence, we partition
[0,1]
into
M
intervals

0=a_{0}<a_{1}<\cdots<a_{M}=1
with
a_{i}-a_{i-1}=1/M
. Then, for any fixed
M
,
we have
\max_{1\leq i\leq M}|\int_{0}^{a_{i}}f_{kl}(s)\mathrm{d}\xi^{n}_{kl}(s)-\int_{0}^{a_{i}}f_{kl}(s)\mathrm{d}\xi_{kl}(s)|\rightarrow 0

as
n\rightarrow+\infty
. Using
\|f_{kl}(s)\|<+\infty
and

\sup_{|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{M}}\Big|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}f_{kl}(s)\mathrm{d}\xi^{n}_{kl}(s)-\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}f_{kl}(s)\mathrm{d}\xi_{kl}(s)\Big|\leq\|f_{kl}\|\sup_{|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{M}}\left\{\Big|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi^{n}_{kl}(s)\Big|+\Big|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi_{kl}(s)\Big|\right\}\rightarrow 0

as
M,n\rightarrow+\infty
by Proposition
3
, we can show the second term of (
41
)
also
\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
. This completes our proof.

Appendix D
Proof of heavy traffic lower bound (Theorem
1
)

The proof closes three exposition gaps in
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 6]
:
(A1) the queue-to-workload translation underlying Eq. (36)
(Section
D.6
);
(A2) continuity of the optimal allocation, used implicitly in Eq. (95)
to pass a Riemann sum to an integral
(Section
D.3
);
and (A3) the vanishing partition mesh, justified here for
\bm{W}_{+}

a reflected Brownian motion
(Section
D.7
).

D.1
Overview

Since the queue based on the predicted classes contains a mixture of true
classes due to misclassification, we must characterize its asymptotic
compositions in order to analyze the queueing cost. For
k,l\in[K]
, let

\widetilde{N}_{kl}^{n}(t),~t\in[0,n]
be the number of true class
k
jobs that are
predicted as class
l
and remain in system
n
at time
t
, and let

\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{kl}^{n}(t):=n^{-1/2}\widetilde{N}_{kl}^{n}(t)
denote its the diffusion-scaled
version. (See Section
C.3
for the formal definition.)

Proposition 4

(Proportion of true class labels)
.

Given a classifier
f_{\theta}
and a sequence of queueing systems,
suppose that Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
hold. Under any work-conserving p-FCFS policy, we have that
for any
k,l\in[K]
and
t\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{kl}^{n}(t)=\frac{p_{k}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}}{\sum_{r=1}^{K}p_{r}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{rl}^{n}}\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{n}_{l}(t)+o_{n}(1).

(42)

For any predicted class
l\in[K]
, Proposition
4
states the
unobservable
(scaled) queue length of true class

k
jobs,
\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{kl}^{n}
, is proportional to the overall queue length

\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{l}^{n}
. Moreover, the proportion is asymptotically “stable” in the
sense that
\frac{p_{k}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}}{\sum_{r=1}^{K}p_{r}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{rl}^{n}}

converges to a constant under Assumption
2
.
Since the actual cost incurred by a job is governed by the job’s true class
label, the decomposition (
42
) enables to approximate the aggregated cost incurred by jobs in predicted
class
l\in[K]
according to their true class labels (see Eq. (
51
) to come for details).

Next, we use Proposition
1
to reveal asymptotic relationships between
endogenous processes such as
\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}^{n}
and
\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{l}^{n}
(e.g., see
Lemma
15
in Section
D
). Combining this with the decomposition (
42
), we establish a link
between the actual cost incurred in the presence of misclassification errors
and the exogenous component
\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{+}^{n}
(see Eq. (
52
) to come). Our analysis allows us to idenfify a lower bound as a
workload allocation over the predicted classes as we characterize in
Proposition
1
.

Discussion of proof

The proof of Proposition
4
is nontrivial,
but once we arrive at the decomposition (
42
), it sheds light on the construction of the
P
c\mu
rule (Definition
3
). The main challenge in deriving the cost
functions (
9
) used in the P
c\mu
rule (Definition
3
)
is the proof of Proposition
4
. We
decompose the stochastic fluctuation
\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{kl}^{n}
into fluctuations of other
processes, including the service process
\bm{\widetilde{S}}_{l}^{n}
and the classification
partial sum process,
\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}^{n}
. Since service times and the true/predicted
class labels are correlated in our model, it is not a priori clear how the
corresponding fluctuations in
\bm{\widetilde{S}}_{l}^{n}
and
\bm{\widetilde{Z}}_{kl}^{n}
jointly influence
that of
\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{kl}^{n}
. The derivation of (
42
) requires articulating the stochastic fluctuation of

\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{kl}^{n}
. Toward this goal, we provide a novel characterization of the
service completion in the predicted classes from the perspective of the common
stream of arrivals in Eq. (
59
). The proof of the proposition is provided in
Section
D.5
.

Assumption
2
is essential to prove Theorem
1
. Proposition
4
relies on Proposition
1
, which builds on the
o(n^{-1/2})
convergence rate in the heavy traffic condition (
3
) following from Assumption
2
. Importantly, the assumption implies
|\widetilde{\mu}_{l}^{n}-\widetilde{\mu}_{l}|=o_{n}(1)~\forall~l\in[K]
, which allows to establish a pivotal relationship between
\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}^{n}
and
\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{l}^{n}
in Lemma
15
. In Section
E.1
, we explain how Assumption
2
also leads to a crucial equivalence between
the age and sojourn time processes, laying the foundation of the optimality of the
P
c\mu
-rule in Theorem
2
to come.

D.1.1
Comparison to the analysis of
Van Mieghem [66]

Plugging
Q^{n}=I
into Theorem
1
, we recover the
classical result under perfect classification in
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition
6]
. In addition to the broader generality of Theorem
1
, our proof addresses key conditions that are missing in
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 6]
and incomplete justifications in their proof, even within the classical setting when all true classes are known.

Convergence of service rate functions

As mentioned in Section
D.1
, the convergence of
\mu_{k}^{n},p_{k}^{n},\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}~\forall~k,l\in[K]
, as stated in Assumption
2
, plays a critical role in establishing Theorem
1
by guaranteeing the convergence of the
service rate

\widetilde{\mu}_{l}^{n}
, which is essential for translating the queue length
\bm{\widetilde{N}}_{l}^{n}
into the workload
\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}^{n}
(Lemma
15
). Similarly, we identify that a comparable convergence condition, e.g.,
(\bar{V}_{k}^{n})^{\prime}-(\bar{V}_{k}^{*})^{\prime}=o_{n}(1)
in their notation, is one of the two alternative sufficient conditions that can complement the argument for the lower bound in
Van Mieghem [66]
.

Without assuming i.i.d. service times, as in our framework, we found that
Van Mieghem [66, Assumption 1]
alone does not suffice to derive
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 3]
, a key intermediate result analogous to our Lemma
15
, which underpins their lower bound proof in
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 6]
. In Section
G
, we provide detailed analysis and identify conditions that can be incorporated into
Van Mieghem [66]
to rigorously establish the results claimed in that work.

Continuity of the optimal workload allocation

In both the classical and our setting,
the optimal workload allocation
h
, which solves (
7
), must be continuous with respect
to the total workload
\bm{W}_{+}(t),~t\in[0,1]
.
This crucial argument was not proven in
Van Mieghem [66]
, making
their results incomplete. We address this gap by proving continuity of
h
in Proposition
14
.

Parition of the time interval

In the proof of Theorem
1
, we partition the time
interval
[0,1]
to bound the accrued cost over each small subinterval, and
the approximation errors due to the finite partitioning is handled accordingly
(see Eq. (
46
)). In the proof
of
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 6]
, however, the partition is chosen by a
different method than ours, and the author claims that the partition size,
hence the approximation error, can be arbitrarily small without justification.
When the workload is a
general reflected process
as
in
Van Mieghem [66]
’s setting, we found this claim to be challenging to
prove. As a result, we provide a rigorous justification for their claim in the
particular case when
\bm{W}_{+}
is a reflected Brownian motion in
Section
D.7
.

D.2
Detailed proof of heavy traffic lower bound
(Theorem
1
)

We begin by proving (
6
).
We analyze

\bm{J}^{n}_{\text{$\pi_{n}$}}(t;Q^{n})
for a fixed
t\in[0,1]
. By definition,

\bm{J}^{n}_{\text{$\pi_{n}$}}(t;Q^{n})=n^{-1}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{K}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{nt}C_{k}^{n}\left(\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(s).

For a fixed
\varepsilon>0
, partition
[0,1]
into

0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{M}=1
such that

\sup_{i}(t_{i+1}-t_{i})=\varepsilon
, where
M
is a constant dependent on

\varepsilon
. Let

\mathrm{d}\xi_{kl,i}^{n}:=\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}}{\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(nt_{i+1})-\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(nt_{i})}
be a probability measure over
[nt_{i},nt_{i+1}]
,
convexity of
C^{n}_{k}
and Jensen’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\bm{J}^{n}_{\text{$\pi_{n}$}}(t;Q^{n})=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{K}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i}\int_{nt_{i}}^{nt_{i+1}}C_{k}^{n}(\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}(s))\mathrm{d}\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(s)

(43)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{k}\sum_{l}\sum_{i}[\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(nt_{i+1})-\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(nt_{i})]\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{kl,i}^{n}}[C_{k}^{n}(\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n})]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{k}\sum_{l}\sum_{i}[\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(nt_{i+1})-\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(nt_{i})]C_{k}^{n}(\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{kl,i}^{n}}[\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}]).

By connecting
\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{kl,i}^{n}}[\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}]
with the workload
process, we can show the following claim. Recall
o_{n}(1)\rightarrow 0

uniformly over
t\in[0,1]
.

Claim 1
.

\displaystyle\bm{J}^{n}_{\text{$\pi_{n}$}}(t;Q^{n})\geq n^{-1}\sum_{k}\sum_{l}\sum_{i}[\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(nt_{i+1})-\widetilde{A}_{kl}^{n}(nt_{i})]C_{k}^{n}(\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{kl,i}^{n}}[\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{n}])

(44)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\sum_{l}\sum_{i}[\lambda p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}(t_{i+1}-t_{i})+o_{n}(1)]\cdot C_{k}^{n}\Big(n^{1/2}\Big[[\widetilde{\rho}_{l}(t_{i+1}-t_{i})]^{-1}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\bm{\widetilde{W}}^{n}_{l}(s)\mathrm{d}s+o_{n}(1)\Big]\Big).

Since
C_{k}^{n}(n^{1/2}\cdot)\rightarrow C_{k}(\cdot)
and
C_{k}^{\prime}
is bounded on the
compact set
[0,2\lim\sup_{n}\|\bm{W}_{+}^{n}\|/\widetilde{\rho}_{l}]
, the right hand side of
inequality (
44
) can be rewritten as
