, it is easy to verify that

\sum_{l\neq l^{n}_{0}}w^{n}_{l}=1,\quad\sum_{l\neq l^{n}_{0}}\widetilde{\rho}_{l}=1-\widetilde{\rho}_{l^{n}_{0}}\leq 1-\widetilde{\rho}_{\min}.

Rearranging the terms, we can claim that there exists some predicted class

k^{n}_{0}
satisfying

w^{n}_{k^{n}_{0}}-\widetilde{\rho}_{k^{n}_{0}}\geq\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\min}}{K-1}
, which is
equivalent to (
72
).

Combining (
72
) and Lemmas
19
,
20
, for
sufficient large
n

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{a}}_{k^{n}_{0}}^{n}(t_{1}+\delta^{n}_{1}(\varepsilon))-\bm{\widetilde{a}}_{k^{n}_{0}}^{n}(t_{1})=

\displaystyle n^{1/2}\delta^{n}_{1}(\varepsilon)-n^{-1/2}\widetilde{\rho}_{k^{n}_{0}}^{-1}\Delta\widetilde{T}^{n}_{k^{n}_{0}}(nt)+\alpha_{0}\delta_{1}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\delta_{1}(\varepsilon)-\widetilde{\rho}_{k^{n}_{0}}^{-1}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\min}}{K-1}+\widetilde{\rho}_{k^{n}_{0}}\Big)\cdot\delta_{1}(\varepsilon)+\alpha_{0}\delta_{1}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-3\alpha_{0}\delta_{1}(\varepsilon)+\alpha_{0}\delta_{1}(\varepsilon)=-2\alpha_{0}\delta_{1}(\varepsilon).

F.4
Proof of Proposition
10

Fix
t_{2}\in[t_{1},(t_{1}+\delta_{1}^{n}(\varepsilon))\wedge 1]
. By symmetry, it
suffices to show

\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{l_{1}}^{n}(t_{2})-\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{l_{2}}^{n}(t_{2})<3\varepsilon/2
for any

l_{1},l_{2}\in[K]
. When
l_{2}
is selected for service by P
c\mu
-rule in

[nt_{1},nt_{2}]
, we can employ a similar analysis as in the proof of
Proposition
8
to show

\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{l_{1}}^{n}(t_{2})-\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{l_{2}}^{n}(t_{2})<\varepsilon
. For the other
case, we have from Lemma
22
that

\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{l_{2}}^{n}(t_{2})-\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{l_{2}}^{n}(t_{1})\geq-\varepsilon/4
, since

\bm{\widetilde{a}}^{n}_{l_{2}}(t_{2})-\bm{\widetilde{a}}^{n}_{l_{2}}(t_{1})\geq 0
. Also, once again by
Lemma
22
, one can check

\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{l_{1}}^{n}(t_{2})-\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{l_{1}}^{n}(t_{1})\leq\varepsilon/4
since

t_{2}-t_{1}\leq\delta^{n}_{1}(\varepsilon)
. Combining equations above yields the
desired result.

Appendix G
Adding to the exposition in classical heavy traffic queueing analysis

In this section, we use our analysis to identify sufficient conditions that address gaps in the original arguments for the lower bound in the classical heavy traffic analysis
Van Mieghem [66]
. In this sense, we observe that the original proofs in
Van Mieghem [66]
leave certain gaps, which our analysis addresses by providing sufficient conditions for the claims to hold rigorously.

When interarrival and service times are i.i.d, the arguments in
Van Mieghem [66]
hold under their original assumptions. However, when interarrival or service times are not i.i.d in the G/G/1 systems, we identify specific sufficient conditions either on
\bar{U}_{k}^{n}
or
\bar{V}_{k}^{n}
under which the original arguments can be rigorously complemented. Based on our analysis, we found it challenging to rigorously address these gaps without using such conditions.

G.1
Conditions for complementing the lower bound proof in
Van Mieghem [66]

We provide conditions under which our analysis complements the original proof for the lower bound in
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 6]
for the G/G/1 systems studied in that work. Specifically, these conditions suffice to prove a key intermediate step,
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 3]
, which is analogous to our Lemma
15
. The convergence is in the almost-sure sense in the uniform metric as in the sample path analysis in
Van Mieghem [66]
.

Claim 3
.

If for each class
k\in[K]
, either (i)
(\bar{V}_{k}^{n})^{\prime}=(\bar{V}_{k}^{*})^{\prime}+o_{n}(1)
or (ii)
\bar{V}_{k}^{n}=\bar{V}_{k}^{*}+o_{n}(n^{-1/2})
is satisfied, then the proof of
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 3]
can be rigorously complemented, allowing the queue lengths to be translated to workloads for deriving the lower bound result in
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 6]
.

When the service times are i.i.d in all systems
n
,
\bar{V}_{k}^{n}
and
\bar{V}_{k}^{*}
are deterministic and linear. In this case, the first condition is satisfied by the convergence of
\bar{V}_{k}^{n}
to
\bar{V}_{k}^{*}
as argued in
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 1]
, and the proofs for
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 3, 6]
apply. Our focus, however, is on G/G/1 systems where the service times are not i.i.d., and thus
\bar{V}_{k}^{n}
may not be linear processes.

Proof of the claim

We argue that having at least one of the two stated conditions is sufficient to prove
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 3]
, a key intermediate result for the lower bound in
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 6]
. Specifically, either condition can be used to derive a variant of Eq (82) from Eq (81) in the proof of
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 3]
. Using the Mean-Value Theorem and the uniform continuity of
(\bar{V}_{k}^{*})^{\prime}
on a compact interval, if condition (i) holds,

\displaystyle n\bar{V}_{k}^{n}\big(n^{-1}A_{k}^{n}(t)\big)-n\bar{V}_{k}^{n}\big(n^{-1}A_{k}^{n}(t)-n^{-1}N_{k}^{n}(t)\big)

\displaystyle=(\bar{V}_{k}^{n})^{\prime}\big(\xi_{k}^{n}(t))\cdot N_{k}^{n}(t)

\displaystyle=(\bar{V}_{k}^{n})^{\prime}\big(n^{-1}A_{k}^{n}(t))\cdot N_{k}^{n}(t)+o(N_{k}^{n}(t))

\displaystyle=(\bar{V}_{k}^{*})^{\prime}\big(n^{-1}A_{k}^{n}(t))\cdot N_{k}^{n}(t)+o(N_{k}^{n}(t))+o_{n}(1),

where
\xi_{k}^{n}(t)\in[n^{-1}A_{k}^{n}(t)-n^{-1}N_{k}^{n}(t),n^{-1}A_{k}^{n}(t)]
. Similarly, if condition (ii) is guaranteed, we have

\displaystyle n\bar{V}_{k}^{n}\big(n^{-1}A_{k}^{n}(t)\big)-n\bar{V}_{k}^{n}\big(n^{-1}A_{k}^{n}(t)-n^{-1}N_{k}^{n}(t)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\bar{V}_{k}^{*}\big(n^{-1}A_{k}^{n}(t)\big)-n\bar{V}_{k}^{*}\big(n^{-1}A_{k}^{n}(t)-n^{-1}N_{k}^{n}(t)\big)+o(n^{1/2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\bar{V}_{k}^{*})^{\prime}\big(n^{-1}A_{k}^{n}(t))\cdot N_{k}^{n}(t)+o(N_{k}^{n}(t))+o(n^{1/2}).

In either case, the right-hand side can be plugged into
Van Mieghem [66, Eq (81)]
, ensuring the proof of
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 3]
is complete. Without either condition, it remains challenging to rigorously derive
Van Mieghem [66, Eq (82)]
or its equivalent form based on our analysis.

G.2
Conditions for complementing the optimality proof in
Van Mieghem [66]
,
Mandelbaum and Stolyar [40]

As mentioned in Sections
1
,
5
, and
E.2
, the optimality proofs in the classical heavy traffic queueing literature
[
66
,
40
]
are incomplete because they did not establish the equivalence between convergence of the scaled age and sojourn time processes. With
Q^{n}=I
, our analysis provides a proof for the equivalence even in the traditional setting with known true classes and fills the gap in the literature. We find two alternative conditions that can guarantee the asymptotic equivalence between age and sojourn time in
Van Mieghem [66]
,
Mandelbaum and Stolyar [40]
.

Claim 4
.

If for each class
k\in[K]
, either (i)
(\bar{U}_{k}^{n})^{\prime}=(\bar{U}_{k}^{*})^{\prime}+o_{n}(1)
or (ii)
\bar{U}_{k}^{n}=\bar{U}_{k}^{*}+o_{n}(n^{-1/2})
, then the age and sojourn time processes in
Van Mieghem [66]
,
Mandelbaum and Stolyar [40]
can be shown asymptotically equivalent in a manner analogous to our Proposition
6
. These conditions, combined with our proof for Proposition
7
with
Q^{n}=I
, complement the original optimality proofs for the G
c\mu
-rule and
D\text{-}Gc\mu
.

Note that the conditions resemble those for the lower bound result in the previous section. In
Mandelbaum and Stolyar [40]
, the interarrival times are i.i.d, so
\bar{U}_{k}^{n}
is linear, and the
o(n^{-1/2})
rate of arrival-rate convergence assumed in
Mandelbaum and Stolyar [40, Eq (2)]
then yields
\bar{U}_{k}^{n}=\bar{U}_{k}^{*}+o_{n}(n^{-1/2})
uniformly on compacts, satisfying condition (ii), and hence condition (i) as well.

Proof of the claim

Plugging
Q^{n}=I
in our framework, Assumptions
2
,
4
,
3
, and
5
and the result of
Proposition
3

satisfy all assumptions in
Van Mieghem [66]
. Thus, the relationship between the age and sojourn time processes in Proposition
6
and the reformulation of the age processes in (
64
), which stemmed from Proposition
3
, apply to the classical setting in
Van Mieghem [66]
with known true classes, where the age processes can be written as
a_{k}^{n}(nt)=nt-U_{k}^{n}(A_{k}^{n}(nt)-N_{k}^{n}(nt)+1)+o_{n}(n^{1/2})
.

Following the proof of Proposition
6
with
Q^{n}=I
, we would have under condition (i) that

\displaystyle\bar{U}^{n}_{k}(n^{-1}(A_{k}^{n}(nt)-N_{k}^{n}(nt)+1))=

\displaystyle\bar{U}^{n}_{k}\big(\bar{A}_{k}^{n}(t)+n^{-1/2}\tilde{A}_{k}^{n}(t)-n^{-1/2}\tilde{N}_{k}^{n}(t)+o(n^{-1/2})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t+(\bar{U}_{k}^{*})^{\prime}(\bar{A}^{n}_{k}(t))\cdot n^{-1/2}\big(\bm{A}_{k}^{n}(t)-\tilde{N}_{k}^{n}(t)\big)+o(n^{-1/2}),

where we use
\bar{U}^{n}_{k}=(\bar{A}_{k}^{n})^{-1}
in the proof of
Van Mieghem [66, Proposition 2]
, the Mean Value Theorem, and the uniform continuity of
(\bar{U}_{k}^{*})^{\prime}
. With condition (ii), we can write similarly that

\displaystyle\bar{U}^{n}_{k}(n^{-1}(A_{k}^{n}(nt)-N_{k}^{n}(nt)+1))=

\displaystyle\bar{U}^{*}_{k}(n^{-1}(A_{k}^{n}(nt)-N_{k}^{n}(nt)+1))+o(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{U}^{*}_{k}\big(\bar{A}_{k}^{n}(t)+n^{-1/2}\tilde{A}_{k}^{n}(t)-n^{-1/2}\tilde{N}_{k}^{n}(t)+o(n^{-1/2})\big)+o(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t+(\bar{U}_{k}^{*})^{\prime}(\bar{A}^{n}_{k}(t))\cdot n^{-1/2}\big(\tilde{A}_{k}^{n}(t)-\tilde{N}_{k}^{n}(t)\big)+o(n^{-1/2}).

Plugging the right-hand side of either case into the remaining proof for Proposition
6
, the desired asymptotic equivalence between age and sojourn time in
Van Mieghem [66]
is established. Under the assumptions in
Van Mieghem [66]
without condition (i) or (ii), it is not evident how to approximate
\bar{U}^{n}_{k}(n^{-1}(A_{k}^{n}(nt)-N_{k}^{n}(nt)+1))
and establish the asymptotic equivalence between the age and sojourn time processes.

Appendix H
Proofs for Section
7

H.1
Proof for Proposition
2

From Theorem
1
,

\bm{J}^{*}(t;Q)=\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{l=1}^{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\lambda p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}C_{k}(\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}(s))\mathrm{d}s
where
\{\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}\}_{l\in[K]}
is characterized
as

\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}=\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}/\widetilde{\rho}_{l},~\forall~l\in[K],\quad\sum_{l}\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}=\bm{W}_{+},\quad\widetilde{\mu}_{l}\widetilde{C}_{l}^{\prime}(\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l})=\widetilde{\mu}_{m}\widetilde{C}_{m}^{\prime}(\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{m}),~\forall~l,m\in[K],

(73)

by our argument in Section
5
.
According to Assumption
6
,
we can equivalently reformulate (
73
) as

\widetilde{\rho}_{l}\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}(t;Q)=\frac{(\beta_{l}(Q))^{-1}}{\sum_{m=1}^{K}(\beta_{m}(Q))^{-1}}\bm{W}_{+}(t),\quad\beta_{l}(Q)=\frac{\widetilde{\mu}_{l}\widetilde{c}_{l}}{\widetilde{\rho}_{l}},~\forall~t\in[0,1],~\forall~l\in[K].

For any
s\in[0,t]
, the integrand

\sum_{l=1}^{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\lambda p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}C_{k}(\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l}(s))

can be written as

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{l=1}^{K}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\lambda p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}\frac{c_{k}}{2}\frac{1}{\widetilde{\rho}_{l}^{2}}\Big(\frac{\bm{W}_{+}(s)}{\sum_{m=1}^{K}\frac{\beta_{l}(Q)}{\beta_{m}(Q)}}\Big)^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\bm{W}_{+}^{2}(s)\sum\limits_{l=1}^{K}\frac{1}{\widetilde{\rho}_{l}^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{\sum_{m=1}^{K}\frac{\beta_{l}(Q)}{\beta_{m}(Q)}}\Big)^{2}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\lambda p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}c_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\bm{W}_{+}^{2}(s)\sum\limits_{l=1}^{K}\frac{\beta_{l}(Q)}{\big(\sum_{m=1}^{K}\frac{\beta_{l}(Q)}{\beta_{m}(Q)}\big)^{2}},

where the last equality holds since
\beta_{l}(Q)=\widetilde{\mu}_{l}\widetilde{c}_{l}/\widetilde{\rho}_{l}
,
\widetilde{c}_{l}=\frac{\sum\lambda p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}c_{k}}{\sum\lambda p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}}
by definition. The summation term can be further written as

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{l=1}^{K}\frac{\beta_{l}(Q)}{\big(\sum_{m=1}^{K}\frac{\beta_{l}(Q)}{\beta_{m}(Q)}\big)^{2}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{K}\frac{\beta_{l}(Q)\big(\prod_{r\neq l}\beta_{r}(Q)\big)^{2}}{\big(\sum_{m=1}^{K}\frac{\beta_{l}(Q)}{\beta_{m}(Q)}\big)^{2}\big(\prod_{r\neq l}\beta_{r}(Q)\big)^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{K}\frac{\beta_{l}(Q)\big(\prod_{r\neq l}\beta_{r}(Q)\big)^{2}}{\big(\sum_{m=1}^{K}\prod_{r\neq m}\beta_{m}(Q)\big)^{2}}=\frac{\prod_{r=1}^{K}\beta_{r}(Q)}{\sum_{m=1}^{K}\prod_{r\neq m}\beta_{r}(Q)}=\frac{1}{\sum_{m=1}^{K}(\beta_{m}(Q))^{-1}}.

Applying a similar approach to the proof of Proposition
7
with the corresponding class indices, the age process converges under the
Naive G
c\mu
-rule, and by Lemma
3
, the cumulative
cost converges to

\bm{J}_{\text{Naive}}(t;Q)=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{t}\lambda p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}C_{k}(\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l,\text{Naive}}(s))\mathrm{d}s,

where

\{\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l,\text{Naive}}\}_{l\in[K]}
is the limit of the sojourn time
process under the Naive G
c\mu
-rule. By similar analysis as in the proof of
Theorem
2
, the limit

\{\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l,\text{Naive}}\}_{l\in[K]}
is characterized by

\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l,\text{Naive}}=\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}/\widetilde{\rho}_{l},~\forall~l\in[K],\quad\sum_{l}\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{l}=\bm{W}_{+},\quad\widetilde{\mu}_{l}C_{l}^{\prime}(\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l,\text{Naive}})=\widetilde{\mu}_{m}C_{m}^{\prime}(\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{m,\text{Naive}}),~\forall~l,m\in[K].

In contrast to Eq. (
73
), each predicted class
l\in[K]
is associated with the

original cost function

C_{l}
in the above characterization, which
does
not
take into account misclassification errors in the marginal
cost rate of the class. It follows that

\widetilde{\rho}_{l}\bm{\widetilde{\tau}}_{l,\text{Naive}}(t;Q)=\frac{(\beta_{l,\text{Naive}}(Q))^{-1}}{\sum_{m=1}^{K}(\beta_{m,\text{Naive}}(Q))^{-1}}\bm{W}_{+}(t),\quad\beta_{l,\text{Naive}}(Q)=\frac{\widetilde{\mu}_{l}c_{l}}{\widetilde{\rho}_{l}},~\forall~l\in[K].

Combining the equations above and noting
\beta_{l}(Q)=\widetilde{\mu}_{l}\widetilde{c}_{l}/\widetilde{\rho}_{l}
, we have

\displaystyle\bm{J}_{\text{Naive}}(t;Q)=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{l=1}^{K}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{t}\lambda p_{k}\widetilde{q}_{kl}\frac{c_{k}}{2\widetilde{\rho}_{l}^{2}}\bm{W}_{+}^{2}(s)\Big(\frac{(\beta_{l,\text{Naive}}(Q))^{-1}}{\sum_{m=1}^{K}(\beta_{m,\text{Naive}}(Q))^{-1}}\Big)^{2}\mathrm{d}s

(74)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{l=1}^{K}\frac{\beta_{l}(Q)}{\big(\sum_{m}\frac{\beta_{l,\text{Naive}}(Q)}{\beta_{m,\text{Naive}}(Q)}\big)^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{W}_{+}^{2}(s)\mathrm{d}s.

Appendix I
Proof of results in Section
8

I.1
Joint convergence of the AI-based triage system

We define the concerned processes below to analyze the AI triage system.

Definition 12

(Arrival processes of the AI-based triage system)
.

Given a classifier
f_{\theta}
, filtering level
z_{\text{FL}}
,
the number of hired reviewers
\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})
,
and a sequence of queueing systems, suppose that
Assumptions
7

and
8
hold. We define the following for any
system
n
, reviewer
r\in\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})
, and time
t\in[0,n]
:

(i)

(Arrival process of the triage system) Let

U^{n}_{0}(t):=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}u_{i}^{n}
be the partial sum of
interarrival times among the first

\lfloor t\rfloor
jobs arriving at the triage system, and

A^{n}_{0}(t)
be the number
of jobs that arrive at the triage system
n
up to time
t
. Moreover, let

\bm{U}^{n}_{0}(t)
,
\bm{A}_{0}^{n}(t)
be the corresponding diffusion-scaled process,
defined as

\bm{U}^{n}_{0}(t)=n^{-1/2}[U^{n}_{0}(nt)-\Lambda_{n}^{-1}\cdot nt],\quad\bm{A}^{n}_{0}(t)=n^{-1/2}[A^{n}_{0}(nt)-\Lambda_{n}\cdot nt],~\forall~t\in[0,1];

(ii)

(Arrival process of jobs filtered out)
For each class
k\in\{1,2\}
,
let
U_{\text{fl},k}^{n}(t)
be the partial sum of interarrival times among the first

\lfloor t\rfloor
class
k
jobs that are filtered out, and

A_{\text{fl},k}^{n}(t)
be the number of class
k
jobs that
are filtered out by the filtering system up to time
t
, i.e.,

A_{\text{fl},k}^{n}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{A^{n}_{0}(t)}\mathbb{I}(f_{\theta}(X^{n}_{i})<z_{\text{FL}})\cdot Y_{ik}^{n},~\forall~k\in\{1,2\}
. Moreover, let

\widetilde{U}_{\text{fl},0}^{n}(t)
and
\bm{A}_{\text{fl},k}^{n}(t)
be the
corresponding diffusion-scaled processes, defined as

\displaystyle\bm{U}_{\text{fl},k}^{n}(t)=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\Big[U_{\text{fl},k}^{n}(nt)-(\Lambda_{n}p^{n}_{k}(1-g^{n}_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})))^{-1}\cdot nt\Big],~\forall~t\in[0,1],~\forall~k\in\{1,2\}

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{\text{fl},k}^{n}(t)=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\Big[A_{\text{fl},k}^{n}(nt)-\Lambda_{n}p^{n}_{k}(1-g^{n}_{k}(z_{\text{FL}}))\cdot nt\Big],~\forall~t\in[0,1],~\forall~k\in\{1,2\};

(iii)

(Arrival process of the queueing system)
Let
U_{\text{ps},0}^{n}(t)
be the partial sum of interarrival times among the first

\lfloor t\rfloor
jobs that pass through the filtering system and arrive at
the queueing system, and

A_{\text{ps},0}^{n}(t)
be the number
of jobs that pass through the filtering system and arrive at the
queueing system up to time
t
, i.e.,

A_{\text{ps},0}^{n}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{A^{n}_{0}(t)}\mathbb{I}(f_{\theta}(X^{n}_{i})\geq z_{\text{FL}})
. Also, let

\bm{U}_{\text{ps},0}^{n}
and
\bm{A}_{\text{ps},0}^{n}
be the corresponding diffusion-scaled arrival process,
defined as

\displaystyle\bm{U}_{\text{ps},0}^{n}(t)=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}[U_{\text{ps},0}^{n}(nt)-nt\cdot(\Lambda_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{2}p^{n}_{k}g^{n}_{k}(z_{\text{FL}}))^{-1}],~\forall~t\in[0,1],

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{\text{ps},0}^{n}(t)=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}[A_{\text{ps},0}^{n}(nt)-nt\cdot\Lambda_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{2}p^{n}_{k}g^{n}_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})],~\forall~t\in[0,1];

(iv)

(Arrival process of each reviewer)
Let
U_{\text{ps},r}^{n}(t):=\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}u^{n}_{s,r}

be the partial sum of interarrival times among the first

\lfloor t\rfloor
jobs that are assigned to reviewer
r
, and

A_{\text{ps},r}^{n}(t)
be the number
of jobs that are assigned to reviewer
r
up to time
t
, i.e.,

A_{\text{ps},r}^{n}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{A_{\text{ps},0}^{n}(t)}B_{jr}
. Moreover,
let
\bm{U}_{\text{ps}}^{n}(t)=\{\bm{U}_{\text{ps},r}^{n}(t)\}_{r\in\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}
,

\bm{A}_{\text{ps}}^{n}(t)=\{\bm{A}_{\text{ps},r}^{n}(t)\}_{r\in\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}

be the corresponding diffusion-scaled arrival process, defined as

\displaystyle\bm{U}_{\text{ps},r}^{n}(t)=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\Big[U_{\text{ps},r}^{n}(nt)-nt\cdot\frac{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}{\Lambda_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{2}p^{n}_{k}g^{n}_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})}\Big],~\forall~t\in[0,1],

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{\text{ps},r}^{n}(t)=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\Big[A_{\text{ps},r}^{n}(nt)-nt\cdot\frac{\Lambda_{n}}{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}\sum_{k=1}^{2}p^{n}_{k}g^{n}_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})\Big],~\forall~t\in[0,1];

(v)

(Split probability) Let
p_{\text{fl},k}^{n}
be the probability that
a job arriving at the triage system is of class
k
and is filtered out by the
filtering system, i.e.,

p_{\text{fl},k}^{n}=p^{n}_{k}(1-g^{n}_{k}(z_{\text{FL}}))
,
and
p_{\text{ps}}^{n}
be the probability that a job arriving at the
triage system passes through the filtering system, i.e.,

p_{\text{ps}}^{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{2}p^{n}_{k}g^{n}_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})
. Moreover, let
p_{\text{fl},k}
and
p_{\text{ps}}
be
the corresponding limiting probability defined as

p_{\text{fl},k}=p_{k}(1-g_{k}(z_{\text{FL}}))
and

p_{\text{ps}}=\sum_{k=1}^{2}p_{k}g_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})
.

(vi)

(Spliting process) Let
\text{Sp}_{\text{fl},k}(t)
be the number of jobs that
are of class
k
and filtered out by the filtering system among the first
\lfloor t\rfloor
jobs
arriving at the triage system,
and
\text{Sp}_{\text{ps},r}(t)
be the number of jobs that are
assigned to reviewer
r
among the first
\lfloor t\rfloor
jobs arriving at the
triage system.
Moreover, let
\textbf{Sp}_{\text{fl}}(t)=\{\textbf{Sp}_{\text{fl},k}(t)\}_{k\in\{1,2\}}
,

\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps}}(t)=\{\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps},r}(t)\}_{r\in\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}
be the
corresponding diffusion-scaled splitting process, defined as

\displaystyle\textbf{Sp}_{\text{fl},k}(t)=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}[\text{Sp}_{\text{fl},k}(nt)-p_{\text{fl},k}^{n}\cdot nt],~\forall~t\in[0,1],~k\in\{1,2\}

\displaystyle\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps},r}(t)=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}[\text{Sp}_{\text{ps},r}(nt)-\frac{p_{\text{ps}}^{n}\cdot nt}{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}],~\forall~t\in[0,1].

Similar to Definition
8
, we define processes above
on
[0,n]
or
[0,1]
for analysis simplicity. These processes can be
naturally extended to
[0,+\infty)
to apply the martingale FCLT
(Lemma
5
)
and FCLT for split processes from
[
71
, Theorem 9.5.1]
,
which yields the joint convergence result below. With a slight abuse of notation,
we adopt Assumption
3
to guarantee
unform integrability of quantities associated with the triage system.

Lemma 26

(Joint convergence of the AI-based triage system)
.

Given a classifier
f_{\theta}
, filtering level
z_{\text{FL}}
, the number of hired reviewers

\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})
, and a sequence of queueing systems, suppose that
Assumptions
3
,
7
, and
8
hold.
Then, we have that:
(i) there exists Brownian motion
(\bm{A}_{0},\textbf{Sp}_{\text{fl}},\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps}})
such that

(\bm{A}^{n}_{0},\textbf{Sp}_{\text{fl}}^{n},\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps}}^{n})\Rightarrow(\bm{A}_{0},\textbf{Sp}_{\text{fl}},\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps}})

in
(D^{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})+3},WJ_{1})
;
(ii) there exist continuous stochastic processes
(\bm{A}_{\text{fl},1},\bm{A}_{\text{fl},2},\bm{A}_{\text{ps}})

such that

(\bm{A}_{\text{fl},1}^{n},\bm{A}_{\text{fl},2}^{n},\bm{A}_{\text{ps}}^{n})\Rightarrow(\bm{A}_{\text{fl},1},\bm{A}_{\text{fl},2},\bm{A}_{\text{ps}}),\quad\text{in }(D^{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})+2},WJ_{1}),

where
\bm{A}_{\text{fl},k}(t)=p_{\text{fl},k}\bm{A}_{0}(t)+\textbf{Sp}_{\text{fl},k}(\Lambda t)

and
\bm{A}_{\text{ps},r}(t)=\frac{p_{\text{ps}}\bm{A}_{0}(t)}{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}+\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps},r}(\Lambda t)
;
(iii) there exists continuous stochastic processes

(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}})
such that

(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}}^{n})\Rightarrow(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}}),\quad\text{in }(D^{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})+2},WJ_{1}).

Proof.

As for (i), according to Assumption
3
,
we have that
\text{Var}[u^{n}_{1}]<+\infty
for each
n
,
and
\text{Var}[u^{n}_{1}]
converges to some constant
\sigma^{2}_{u}
.
Then, by martingale FCLT (Lemma
5
),
it is easy to show that

(\bm{U}^{n}_{0},\textbf{Sp}_{\text{fl}}^{n},\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps}}^{n})
jointly convrges to

(\bm{U}_{0},\textbf{Sp}_{\text{fl}},\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps}})
. Here,

\bm{U}_{0}
is a zero-drift Brownian motion with variance being some

\sigma^{2}_{u}
,
and
\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps}}
is a zero-drift Bronian motion with covariance matrix being

\Sigma=(\sigma^{2}_{r_{1},r_{2}})
, where

\sigma^{2}_{r_{1},r_{1}}=\frac{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})-1}{\Gamma^{2}(z_{\text{FL}})}
and

\sigma^{2}_{r_{1},r_{2}}=-\frac{1}{\Gamma^{2}(z_{\text{FL}})},~\forall~r_{1}\neq r_{2}
.
According to
[
71
, Corollary 13.8.1]
, the joint convergence
of
(\bm{A}_{0},\textbf{Sp}_{\text{fl}},\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps}})
follows immediately. (ii) is a direct consequence
of (i) and
[
71
, Theorem 9.5.1]
. Then, by
[
71
, Corollary 13.8.1]
,
(iii) is a corollary of (ii).
∎

With a slight abuse of notation, we extend from Definition
8

and Section
3
in order to define
\widetilde{Z}^{n}_{kl,r}
,
\widetilde{R}^{n}_{l,r}
,

V_{\text{ps},r}^{n}
on the jobs that are assigned to each reviewer
r
.
Let
\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n}:=\{\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n}_{kl,r}\}_{k,l\in\{1,2\},r\in[\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})]}
,

\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n}:=\{\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n}_{l,r}\}_{l\in\{1,2\},r\in[\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})]}
,
\bm{V}_{\text{ps}}^{n}:=\{\bm{V}_{\text{ps},r}^{n}\}_{r\in[\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})]}

be the corresponding diffusion-scaled processes.
As the job assignment process is independent of any other random objects by Assumption
8
, it is easy to show
that
\{(\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n}_{kl,r},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n}_{l,r},\bm{V}_{\text{ps},r}^{n})\}
are i.i.d. processes
across all reviewers. Therefore,
by independence and Lemma
9
, we can extend
Lemma
8
to achieve joint convergence of

(\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}}^{n})
over all reviewers.

Lemma 27

(Joint weak convergence of the AI-based triage system I)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3
,
7
, and
8
hold.
Then, there exist Brownian motions
(\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}})
such that

(\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}}^{n})\Rightarrow(\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}}),\quad\text{in }(D^{7\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})},WJ_{1}).

Next, we claim that
(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}}^{n})
and

(\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}}^{n})
are independent processes under
Assumption
7
.
Recall that by Definition
14
,

U_{\text{ps},0}^{n}(t)
denotes the partial sum of interarrival times among the first

\lfloor t\rfloor
jobs that pass throught the filtering system.
Let
\{u^{n}_{j}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}
and

\{(X^{n}_{j},v^{n}_{j},Y^{n}_{j}):j\in\mathbb{N}\}
be the interarrival time
and tuples for jobs that
pass through the filtering system
. Then, we have that

U_{\text{ps},0}^{n}(t):=\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}u^{n}_{j}
.

We first argue that
\{u^{n}_{j}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}
and

\{(X^{n}_{j},v^{n}_{j},Y^{n}_{j}):j\in\mathbb{N}\}
are independent.
Let
\{u_{i}^{n}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
and
\{(X^{n}_{i},v^{n}_{i},Y^{n}_{i}):i\in\mathbb{N}\}

be the interarrival times and tuples for all jobs arriving at the triage system.
Note that the primitive sequences
\{u_{i}^{n}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
and

\{(X^{n}_{i},v^{n}_{i},Y^{n}_{i}):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
are independent by
Assumption
7
(ii).
Therefore, by construction,
\{u^{n}_{j}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}
are the thinned
interarrival times from
\{u_{i}^{n}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
, where each arriving job is
retained independetly with equal probability
p_{\text{ps}}^{n}
.
Moreover, since
\{(X^{n}_{i},v^{n}_{i},Y^{n}_{i}):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
are i.i.d. by
Assumption
7
(i),

\{(X^{n}_{j},v^{n}_{j},Y^{n}_{j}):j\in\mathbb{N}\}
are also i.i.d., following the
conditional distribution
(X^{n}_{1},v^{n}_{1},Y^{n}_{1})\mid f_{\theta}(X^{n}_{1})\geq z_{\text{FL}}
.
It is important to note that the realization of
u^{n}_{j}
can not provide additional information on
a job that is known to have been retained, i.e.,
f_{\theta}(X^{n}_{i})\geq z_{\text{FL}}
), and the tuple
(X^{n}_{j},v^{n}_{j},Y^{n}_{j})
by
Assumption
7
(ii).

According to analysis above,
U_{\text{ps},0}^{n}(t)
and

\{(X^{n}_{j},v^{n}_{j},Y^{n}_{j}):j\in\mathbb{N}\}
are independent, as the
former is a function of
\{u^{n}_{j}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
Let
\{(X^{n}_{s,r},v^{n}_{s,r},Y^{n}_{s,r}):s\in\mathbb{N}\}

be the tuples for jobs assigned to some reviewer
r
, which is split from
\{(X^{n}_{j},v^{n}_{j},Y^{n}_{j}):j\in\mathbb{N}\}

according to the reviewer assignment
\{\mathbf{B}^{n}_{j}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
Since
\{\mathbf{B}^{n}_{j}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}

is independent of any other random objects by
Assumption
7
,
we can adopt a similar approach as above to establish
independence between
(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}}^{n})
and

\{(X^{n}_{s,r},v^{n}_{s,r},Y^{n}_{s,r}):s\in\mathbb{N},r\in[\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})]\}
, which further yields independence between

(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}}^{n})
and
(\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}}^{n})
. Finally, according to
Lemmas
9
,
26
, and
27
,
such independence leads to
the joint weak convergence of the AI triage system below
(Lemma
28
),
which extends Lemma
1
.

Lemma 28

(Joint weak convergence of the AI-based triage system II)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3
,
7
, and
8
hold. Then, we have that

(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}}^{n})\Rightarrow(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}}),\quad\text{in }(D^{8\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})+2},WJ_{1}).

Similarly to Lemma
4
, we can then
strengthen the convergence to uniform topology and conduct
sample path analysis on copies of the original processes. With a slight abuse
of notation, we still use

(\Omega_{\text{copy}},\mathcal{F}_{\text{copy}},\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}})
to denote the
common probability space.

Lemma 29

(Uniform Convergence of the AI Triage System)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
7
,
8
, and
3
hold. Then, there
exist stochastic processes
(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}}),~\forall~n\geq 1
and

(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}})
defined on a common probability
space
(\Omega_{\text{copy}},\mathcal{F}_{\text{copy}},\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}})

such that

(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}}),~\forall~n\geq 1
and

(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}})
are identical in distribution to
their original counterparts and

(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2}^{n},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{Z}}^{n},\bm{\widetilde{R}}^{n},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}})\rightarrow(\bm{U}_{\text{fl},1},\bm{U}_{\text{fl},2},\bm{U}_{\text{ps}},\bm{\widetilde{Z}},\bm{\widetilde{R}},\bm{V}_{\text{ps}}),\quad\text{in }(D^{8\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})+2},\|\cdot\|),\quad\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}}-a.s..

I.2
Sample path analysis of each reviewer

In this section, we conduct sample path analysis for each reviewer.
We adopt a similar analysis approach as in
Section
4

and
5
.
In particular, we consider copies of the original processes
defined on the common probability space
\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}}
, as shown
in Lemma
29
.
We establish all subsequent results regarding almost sure convergence for
the copied processes, which can then be converted back into corresponding
weak convergence results for the original processes.

Heavy Traffic Condition for Each Reviewer

We first show that our
Assumptions
7

and
8
are compatible with
Assumptions
1

and
2

we adopt for each single-server queueing system.

Definition 13
.

Given a classifier
f_{\theta}
, filtering level
z_{\text{FL}}
, toxicity level
z_{\text{TX}}
, the number of hired reviewers
\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})
, and a sequence of queueing systems, suppose that
Assumptions
7

and
8
hold. We define the following for
any system
n
and reviewer
r
:

(i)

(Class prevalence) Let
p^{n}_{k,r}(z_{\text{FL}})
be the conditional probability
that a job that passes through the filtering system and is assigned to reviewer
r

is of class
k
, i.e.,

p^{n}_{k,r}(z_{\text{FL}}):=\mathbb{P}^{n}[Y_{1k,r}^{n}=1\mid f_{\theta}(X^{n}_{1,r})\geq z_{\text{FL}}]
.
Moreover, let
p_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})
be the limiting probability, defined as

p_{k}(z_{\text{FL}}):=\frac{p_{k}g_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})}{p_{1}g_{1}(z_{\text{FL}})+p_{2}g_{2}(z_{\text{FL}})}
;

(ii)

(Confusion matrix) Let
\widetilde{q}^{n}_{kl,r}(\mathbf{z})
be the conditional
probability that a class
k
job arriving at reviewer
r
is predicted as class

l
, i.e.,

\widetilde{q}^{n}_{kl,r}(\mathbf{z}):=\mathbb{P}^{n}[\widetilde{Y}^{n}_{1l,r}=1\mid f_{\theta}(X^{n}_{1,r})\geq z_{\text{FL}},Y_{1k,r}^{n}=1]
.
Moreover, let
\widetilde{q}_{kl}(\mathbf{z})
be the limiting probability, defined as

\widetilde{q}_{k1}(z_{\text{FL}},z_{\text{TX}})=\frac{g_{k}(z_{\text{TX}})}{g_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})}
,

\widetilde{q}_{k2}(z_{\text{FL}},z_{\text{TX}})=\frac{g_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})-g_{k}(z_{\text{tx}})}{g_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})},~\forall~k\in\{1,2\}
;

(iii)

(Arrival rate) Let

\lambda^{n}_{r}=\frac{\Lambda_{n}}{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}[p^{n}_{1}g^{n}_{1}(z_{\text{FL}})+p^{n}_{2}g^{n}_{2}(z_{\text{FL}})]
be the arrival rate of jobs assigned to reviewer
r
. Moreover, let

\lambda=\frac{\Lambda}{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}[p_{1}g_{1}(z_{\text{FL}})+p_{2}g_{2}(z_{\text{FL}})]
be the limiting arrival rate.

We define the arrival rate
\lambda^{n}_{r}
based on
Lemma
26
, which shows that

n^{-1}A_{\text{ps},r}^{n}(nt)=\frac{\Lambda_{n}t}{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{2}p_{k}^{n}g^{n}_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})+o_{n}(1)
.
According to Assumptions
7

and
8
, it is easy to verify that
class prevalence, confusion matrix, and arrival rate all converges to
their limiting values at the rate of
n^{1/2}
.

Lemma 30
.

Given a classifier
f_{\theta}
, filtering level
z_{\text{FL}}
, toxicity level
z_{\text{TX}}
, the number of hired reviewers
\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})
, and a sequence of queueing systems, suppose that
Assumptions
7

and
8
hold. Then,
for any
k,l\in\{1,2\}
, an reveiwer
r\in[\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})]
, we have that

n^{1/2}(\lambda^{n}_{r}-\lambda)\rightarrow 0,\quad n^{1/2}(p^{n}_{k,r}(z_{\text{FL}})-p_{k}(z_{\text{FL}}))\rightarrow 0,\quad n^{1/2}(\widetilde{q}^{n}_{kl,r}(\mathbf{z})-\widetilde{q}_{kl}(\mathbf{z}))\rightarrow 0.

As a direct corollary of Lemma
30

and Assumption
8
(ii), for each reviewer
r
, their limiting traffic intensity
satisfies

\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{2}\frac{p_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})}{\mu_{k}}=\frac{\Lambda}{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}[p_{1}g_{1}(z_{\text{FL}})+p_{2}g_{2}(z_{\text{FL}})]\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{2}\frac{p_{k}g_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})}{\mu_{k}(p_{1}g_{1}(z_{\text{FL}})+p_{2}g_{2}(z_{\text{FL}}))}=1.

Therefore, all reviewers operate under heavy traffic conditions, each of which satisfies
Assumption
1

and
2
. This enables us to directly apply results
for the single-server queueing system to each reviewer. Since the analysis is
similar, we only present the main results below and skip proof details.

Endogenous Processes of the AI-based Triage System

We define the concerned endogenous processes below to analyze the
AI-based triage system following Definition
11
.

Definition 14

(Endogenous processes of the AI-based triage system)
.

Given the filtering level
z_{\text{FL}}
, toxicity level
z_{\text{TX}}
,
and the number of hired reviewers
\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})
, we define the following processes
associated with reviewer
r
in system
n
:

(i)

(Input process for predicted classes) Let
\widetilde{L}_{l,r}^{n}(t)
be the total service time
requested by all jobs predicted as class
l
by time

t\in[0,n]
, i.e.,
\widetilde{L}_{l,r}^{n}(t)=\sum_{s=1}^{A_{\text{ps},r}^{n}(t)}\widetilde{Y}_{sl,r}^{n}v_{s,r}^{n}
,
t\in[0,n]
. Moreover, let
\bm{\widetilde{L}}_{l,r}^{n}(t)
be the corresponding diffusion-scaled process, defined as

\bm{\widetilde{L}}_{l,r}^{n}(t)=n^{-1/2}\Big[\widetilde{L}_{l,r}^{n}(nt)-\frac{\Lambda^{n}}{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}g^{n}_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})}{\mu^{n}_{k}}\widetilde{q}_{kl}^{n}(\mathbf{z})\cdot nt\Big],~t\in[0,1].

(ii)

(Cumulative total input process) Let

L_{+}^{n}(t;\mathbf{z},r)=\sum_{l}\widetilde{L}_{l,r}^{n}(t),t\in[0,n]
be the
cumulative total input process and

\bm{L}_{+}^{n}(t;\mathbf{z},r):=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\bm{\widetilde{L}}_{l,r}^{n}(t),~t\in[0,1]
be the
corresponding diffusion-scaled process, i.e.,

\bm{L}_{+}^{n}(t;\mathbf{z},r)=n^{-1/2}\Big[L_{+}^{n}(nt;\mathbf{z},r)-\frac{\Lambda^{n}}{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p^{n}_{k}g^{n}_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})}{\mu^{n}_{k}}\cdot nt\Big],~\forall~t\in[0,1].

(iii)

(Policy process) Let
\widetilde{T}_{l,r}^{n}(t)
be total amount of service time dedicated to predicted class
l
in
[0,t]
.

(iv)

(Remaining workload process) Let
\widetilde{W}_{l,r}^{n}(t)
be the remaining service
time requested by jobs predicted as class
l
and present—waiting for
service or being served—at time
t\in[0,n]
,

\widetilde{W}_{l,r}^{n}(t)=\widetilde{L}_{l,r}^{n}(t)-\widetilde{T}_{l,r}^{n}(t),\quad t\in[0,n].

and
\bm{\widetilde{W}}^{n}_{l,r}(t):=n^{-1/2}\widetilde{W}^{n}_{l,r}(nt),~\forall~t\in[0,1]
be the
corresponding diffusion scaled process.

(v)

(Total remaining workload process) Let

W_{+}^{n}(t;\mathbf{z},r)=\sum_{l}\widetilde{W}_{l,r}^{n}(t)
be the total remaining workload p
rocess and
\bm{W}_{+}^{n}(t;\mathbf{z},r):=n^{-1/2}\sum_{l=1}^{K}\widetilde{W}^{n}_{l}(nt;\mathbf{z},r),~\forall~t\in[0,1]

be the corresponding diffusion scaled process.

Extending Lemma
13
and Proposition
1
,
we have the following results for the endogenous processes of each reviewer
r
.

Lemma 31

(Convergence of
\bm{L}_{+}^{n}(t;\mathbf{z},r)
and
\bm{W}_{+}^{n}(t;\mathbf{z},r)
)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3
,
7
, and
8
hold.

(i)

for a sequence of feasible policies
\{\text{$\pi_{n}$}\}
,
we have that for each reviewer
r
,

\displaystyle\bm{L}_{+}^{n}(\cdot;\mathbf{z},r)\rightarrow\bm{L}_{+}(\cdot;\mathbf{z},r)~\text{in }(\mathcal{D},\|\cdot\|)~\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}}\text{-a.s.},~\text{where}

\displaystyle\bm{L}_{+}(t;\mathbf{z},r):=\bm{V}_{\text{ps},r}\Big(\frac{\Lambda t}{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}[p_{1}g_{1}(z_{\text{FL}})+p_{2}g_{2}(z_{\text{FL}})]\Big)+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\frac{p_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})}{\mu_{k}}\bm{A}_{\text{ps},r}(t),~t\in[0,1].

(ii)

for a sequence of work-conserving p-FCFS feasible policy,
we have that for each reviewer
r
,

\bm{W}_{+}^{n}(\cdot;\mathbf{z},r)\rightarrow\bm{W}_{+}(\cdot;\mathbf{z},r):=\phi(\bm{L}_{+}(\cdot;\mathbf{z},r))
in
(\mathcal{D},\|\cdot\|)~\mathbb{P}_{\text{copy}}-a.s.
, where
\phi
is the reflection mapping.

Starting from Lemma
31
, we
can then follow the sample path analysis and establish
Theorem
3
in reviewer-level; we skip the detailed
proof here.

I.3
Simulation of the total cost of the AI-based Triage System

As shown in Theorem
3
,
the limiting total cost is solely determined by (i) the limiting exogenous quantities,
such as arrival rate
\Lambda
,
class prevalence
p_{k}(z_{\text{FL}})
, confusion matrix
\widetilde{q}_{kl}(z_{\text{FL}})
, etc; and
(ii) the limiting total workload process
\bm{W}_{+}(\cdot;\mathbf{z},r)
.
Though (i) can be easily estimated, (ii) requires a more detailed analysis to
assist a practical estimation.

According to Lemma
31
,

\bm{W}_{+}(\cdot;\mathbf{z},r)
is a continuous stochastic process. Therefore, it
suffices to approximate the integral by a Riemann sum, using the explicit characterization of
\bm{W}_{+}(t;\mathbf{z},r)
in the lemma.

To that end, we analyze
\bm{A}_{\text{ps},r}(t)
and
\bm{V}_{\text{ps},r}
separately.
By Lemma
26
,
we have that

\bm{A}_{\text{ps},r}(t)=\frac{p_{\text{ps}}\bm{A}_{0}(t)}{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}+\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps},r}(\Lambda t)
.
Note that
\bm{U}_{0}
is a zero-drift Brownian motion with variance being

\sigma^{2}_{u}<+\infty
by Assumption
3
, which can be estimated similarly as in Section
B.2.1
. Then, by
[
71
, Corollary 13.8.1]
, we have that

\bm{A}_{0}(t)=-\bm{U}_{0}(\Lambda t)
and

\bm{A}_{\text{ps},r}(t)=-\frac{\Lambda p_{\text{ps}}}{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})}\bm{U}_{0}(\Lambda t)+\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps},r}(\Lambda t).

Here,
\textbf{Sp}_{\text{ps}}
is a zero-drift Bronian motion with covariance matrix being

\Sigma=(\sigma^{2}_{r_{1},r_{2}})
, where

\sigma^{2}_{r_{1},r_{1}}=\frac{\Gamma(z_{\text{FL}})-1}{\Gamma^{2}(z_{\text{FL}})}
and

\sigma^{2}_{r_{1},r_{2}}=-\frac{1}{\Gamma^{2}(z_{\text{FL}})},~\forall~r_{1}\neq r_{2}
; see discussion following
[
71
, Theorem 9.5.1]
.
For
\bm{V}_{\text{ps},r}
, according to Assumption
3
, it
is easy to verify that
\text{Var}[v^{n}_{s,r}]<+\infty
for each
n
and
converges to some constant
\sigma^{2}_{v}(z_{\text{FL}})=\alpha_{v,1}p_{1}(z_{\text{FL}})+\alpha_{v,2}p_{2}(z_{\text{FL}})-\big(\frac{1}{\mu_{1}}p_{1}(z_{\text{FL}})+\frac{1}{\mu_{2}}p_{2}(z_{\text{FL}})\big)^{2}
.
Then, by martingale FCLT (Lemma
5
), we have that

\bm{V}_{\text{ps},r}
is a zero-drift Brownian motion with variance being
\sigma^{2}_{v}(z_{\text{FL}})
.

In this way, we rewrite
\bm{W}_{+}(t;\mathbf{z},r)
as a function of
(multi-dimensional) Brownian motion, whose Riemann sum can be easily simulated.

Appendix J
Numerical Experiments for the AI-based triage system

Our formulation trades off multiple desiderata, in contrast to the standard
industry practice that choose
z
solely based on prediction metrics,
such as maximizing recall subject to a fixed high precision
level
[
10
]
. To compare our proposed approach with such standard
triage design approaches, we consider the 2-class content moderation problem
described in Section
7
.
We assume the covariates
for positive and negative classes are generated in the same fasion as in the
2d logistic regression problem in
Section
7
, and
consider the logistic regression classifier
f_{\theta}
developed by minimizing
the equally-weighted cross-entropy loss (
w_{1}=1
,
w_{2}=1
).
For simplicity, we fix the toxicity level

z_{\text{TX}}=0.5
and only study how filtering level
z_{\text{FL}}
affects the total cost.

We examine the setting where the positive class has a relatively high arrival rate
to mimic the setting where only flagged content is sent to the triage system, which
results in a relatively high proportion of positive class;
recall Figure
1
.
In particular, we set
[\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}]=[10000,40000]
,

[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]=[50,200]
, where

[\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}]
is the arrival rate of positive and negative classes to
the triage system, and
[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]
is the common service rate for the positive
and negative classes across all reviewers. We consider three cases:
(i) filtering costs dominate, (ii) hiring costs dominate,
and (iii) a trade-off between filtering cost and hiring costs.
The filtering costs and hiring costs are set as follows:
(i)
[c_{\text{FL},1},c_{\text{FL},2}]=[200,-3]
,
c_{r}=800
,
(ii)
[c_{\text{FL},1},c_{\text{FL},2}]=[20,-3]
,
c_{r}=8000
,
and (iii)
[c_{\text{FL},1},c_{\text{FL},2}]=[20,-3]
,
c_{r}=800
.
In all cases, the misclassification costs are set as

[c_{\text{fp}},c_{\text{fn}},c_{\text{tp}},c_{\text{tn}}]=[3,3,-3,-3]
,
and the delay costs are set as

C_{\cdot}(t)=c_{\cdot}t^{2}/2
with
c_{1}=15,c_{2}=1
.

Our goal is to find the best filtering level
z_{\text{FL}}
that minimizes the total
cost. We compare our method that minimizes (
19
) to the following classical method from
Chandak [10]
,
which finds the filtering level
z_{\text{FL}}
by maximizing recall subject to a high
precision level lower bound
z_{\text{prec}}\in[0,1]
.
2
2

2

We follow notations
used in
Chandak [10]
. For the filtering system, precision and recall
are calculated by treating safe content as the positive class.

Both methods can be effectively implemented using small
set of validation data and a linear search.
We set the search range for
z_{\text{FL}}
as
[0.05,0.48]
.

(i) Filtering costs dominate

(ii) Hiring costs dominate

(iii) Trade-off between

filtering and hiring costs

Figure 10:
For different methods, we consider the selected filtering level
z_{\text{FL}}

and present the associated estimated total cost of the AI-based triage system.
The classical method maximizes recall subject to the
precision level
[0.93,0.94,0.95,0.96,0.97]
, positioned from left to right.
This method exhibits highly varying total cost even at high precision levels, making
it hard to determine the best filtering level. In contrast, our method effectively
minimizes the total cost by cheap simulations of (reflected) Brownian motion.

In Figure
10
, we present the average total cost
over 10K sample paths of the simulated (reflected) Brownian motion, with

2\times
the standard error encapsulated in the orange brackets. For the
classical method, we set the precision level as

[0.93,0.94,0.95,0.96,0.97]
, positioned from left to right.
To facilitate comparison with our method, we normalize the estimated
total cost by that of our method. We observe that the classical method
exhibits highly varying total cost (by
\sim 10\%-150\%
) even at high precision
levels in Figure
10
. This demonstrates the importance of
selecting the right filtering level to minimize the total cost. In addition,
for the classical method, it also shows the total cost is highly sensitive to
the precision level. Therefore, the precision level serves as an important
hyperparameter, and it is challenging to determine the best precision
level that corresponds to optimal filtering level using the classical method.

Such challenge arises since our method takes a holistic view of the entire
triage system, yet the classical method only considers the prediction metrics.
In our toy example, a higher precision level leads to a lower selected filtering
level
z_{\text{FL}}
, which results in lower filtering costs and higher hiring costs.
Figure
10
(i)(ii) corresponds to
simpler settings where the total cost aligns with prediction metrics.
That is, when filtering costs or hiring costs dominate, the total cost is
monotone with respect to the filtering level and thus the precision level, as shown
in Figure
10
(i)(ii). Therefore, when adopting the
classical method, we can simply choose the precision level at the search boundary,
which yields a filtering level near the search boundary that minimizes
the total cost. In contrast, in Figure
10
(iii), where
there is a trade-off between filtering and hiring costs, the total cost is
non-monotone and “U”-shaped with respect to the precision/filtering level.
In this case, prediction metrics fails to capture the total cost. While
hyperparameter (precision) tuning based on total costs is possible,
the classical method merely shifts our search space to hyperparameters
(precision levels). In other words, hyperparameter tuning is equivalent
to a naive line search for the decision variable (filtering level
z_{\text{FL}}
)
based on simulated total cost and the classical method does not serve as
effective objectives/metrics. More importantly, without our
Theorem
3
,
the total cost can only be estimated through multiple costly simulations
of the entire triage system.
Our method, in contrast, effectively identifies the correct objective and
finds the best filtering level through cheap simulations of
(reflected) Brownian motion.

Our numerical experiments demonstrate the
the effectiveness of our method and the importance of taking a holistic
view of the entire content moderation system. We hope our method paves the
way for more advanced system designs for complex AI-based triage systems in
practical use.
